in Wyner–Ziv coding to reduce the rate necessary to send ˆyn
1 (j). The bin
index is sent to the receiver in block j + 1 via xn
1(j + 1)

◦At the end of block j + 1, the receiver decodes xn

and xn

Extensions

• The compress–forward lower bound (before maximization) is not in general
convex in p(x)p(x1)p(ˆy1|y1, x1). As such, the lower bound can be improved via
time-sharing [9] to obtain the time-sharing compress–forward bound

C ≥

I(X; Y, ˆY1|X1, Q)
�

• Compress–forward can be combined with partial decode–forward to yield a lower
bound on the capacity of the relay channel [2, Theorem 7]

Relay With Lookahead

• The discrete-memoryless relay with lookahead channel
(X, X1, p(y1|x)p(y|x, x1, y1), Y, Y1, d) [12] consists of four finite alphabets
X, X1, Y, Y1, a collection of conditional pmfs p(y1|x)p(y|x, x1, y1) on Y, Y1,
and lookhead d ∈Z in relay encoding. Recall that the conditional pmf for the
DM-RC is defined as p(y, y1|x, x1). The restriction on the conditional pmf for
the relay-with-lookahead channel is needed to prevent instantaneous or
lookahead dependency of X1 on Y1

Xi

X1i(Y i+d
1
)

p(y1|x)p(y|x, x1, y1)
Yi

Y1i

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)
Page 17 – 67

• The channel is memoryless in the sense that

p(yn
1 |xn)p(yn|xn, xn
1, yn
1 ) =

n
�

i=1
pY1|X(y1i|xi+d)pY |X,X1,Y1(yi|xi, x1i, y1i),

where the pmfs with symbols that do not have positive time indices or time
indices greater than n are arbitrary

• A (2nR, n) code for the relay-with-lookahead channel consists of:

1. A message set [1 : 2nR]

2. An encoder that assigns to a codeword xn(m) to each message m ∈[1 : 2nR]

3. A relay encoder that assigns a symbol x1i(yi+d
1
) to each sequence yi+d
1
for
i ∈[1 : n]

4. A decoder that assigns an estimate ˆm(yn) or an error message e to each
received sequence yn

• The message M ∼Unif[1 : 2nR]

• The definition of probability of error, achievability, and capacity Cd are as before

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)
Page 17 – 68


• The lookahead in the above relay model may be, for example, due to a difference
between the arrival times of the signal from the sender to the receiver and to the
relay. If the signal arrives at both the relay and receiver at the same time, we
have a lookahead of d = −1. In general, if the signal arrives at the receiver
d + 1 transmissions after it arrives at the relay, we have a lookahead of d

• Remarks:

◦Since lookahead d = −1 corresponds to the (classical) DM-RC model,
C−1 = C, the capacity of the DM-RC

◦The channel with d = 0 will be referred to as the DM-RC without delay
(DM-RWD)

◦Cd is monotonically nondecreasing in d

• We define the DM-RC with unlimited lookahead as a relay channel where the
relay encoder can depend noncausally on the entire relay received sequence yn
1
and denote its capacity by C∞. The purpose of studying this extreme case is to
understand the limits on relaying with arbitrary lookahead

• Cd for any finite d and C∞are not known in general

• We present results for the DM-RC with unlimited lookahead and the DM-RWD

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)

Relay With Unlimited Lookahead

• We show that C∞can be strictly larger than the capacity of the DM-RC, C,
and can exceed the cutset bound

• Upper bound on C∞:

Theorem 6 (Unlimited-Lookahead Cutset Bound) [12]: The following is an
upper bound on the capacity of the DM-RC with unlimited lookahead

C∞≤max
p(x,x1) min{I(X, X1; Y ), I(X; Y1) + I(X; Y |X1, Y1)}

• Noncausal decode–forward lower bound [12]:

C∞≥max
p(x,x1) min{I(X; Y1), I(X, X1; Y )}

To prove achievability fix a pmf p(x, x1) and randomly generate 2nR sequence
pairs (xn, xn
1)(m), m ∈[1 : 2nR], each according to �n
i=1 p(xt, x1i)

Since the relay knows yn
1 in advance, it decodes m before transmission
commences. By the LLN and the packing lemma, the probability of error →0
as n →∞if R < I(X; Y1) −δ(ǫ)

The sender and relay then cooperatively transmit (xn, xn
1)(m)

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)
Page 17 – 70


The receiver can reliably decode m if R < I(X, X1; Y ). Combining the two
bounds completes the proof

• The above lower bound is tight for the degraded DM-RC with unlimited
lookahead, where X →(X1, Y1) →Y form a Markov chain. The converse
follows from the upper bound on C∞

• Example: Consider the Sato relay channel [4] with unlimited lookahead. We
know that C = 1.161878 bits/transmission and coincides with the cutset bound

Instantaneous Relaying Lower Bound

• Note that any lower bound on the capacity of the DM-RC, e.g., using partial
decode–forward or compress–forward, is a lower bound on the capacity of the
DM-RWD. We expect, however, that higher rates can be achieved by depending
on present in addition to past received relay symbols

• In instantaneous relaying the relay symbol at time i is a function only of y1i.
This simple scheme yields the lower bound
C0 ≥
max
p(x), x1(y1) I(X; Y )

• We now show that instantaneous relaying can be optimal and can achieve a
higher rate than the cutset bound

Example: Again consider the Sato relay channel. We have shown that
C = 1.161878 bits/transmission and
C∞= log(9/4) = 1.169925 bits/transmission
Consider the instantaneous relaying lower bound with pmf (3/9, 2/9, 4/9) on X
and a mapping from X to X1 of 0 →0, 1 →1, 2 →1

It can be easily shown that these choices yield I(X; Y ) = 1.169925
bits/transmission. Thus the capacity of the Sato relay channel without delay
C0 = C∗
∞= 1.169925 bits/transmission

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)
Page 17 – 74


• Remarks:

◦This result is not too surprising. Since the channel from the sender to the
relay is noiseless, complete cooperation, which requires knowledge of the
entire received sequence in advance, can be achieved simply via instantaneous
relaying

◦Since for this example C0 > C and C coincides with the cutset bound, this
result shows that instantaneous relaying alone can achieve a higher rate than
the cutset bound!

• Superposition of instantaneous relaying with partial decode–forward yield the
lower bound

C0 ≥
max
p(u,v,x), x1(v,y1) min{I(V, X; Y ), I(U; Y1|V ) + I(X; Y |V, U)},

which is optimal for degraded and semi-deterministic RWD channels

• Remarks:

◦The above result shows that amplify–forward alone can be optimal for both
the AWGN RWD and the AWGN relay with unlimited lookahead. This is
surprising given the extreme simplicity of this scheme

◦Combining results, we have shown that the capacity of the AWGN relay with
unlimited lookahead is known if g1 ≥(g + g2) or if
g2
1 ≤g2
2 min{1, P/(g2
1P + 1)}. Note that the capacity in this range coincides
with the cutset bound

◦It can be shown using superposition of amplify–forward and decode–forward
that the capacity of the AWGN RWD can exceed the cutset bound

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)
Page 17 – 77

Coherent Cooperation

• In studying the relay channel with and without delay, we have introduced three
types of coherent cooperation:

◦Decode–forward: Here the relay decodes part or all of the message and the
sender and relay cooperate on sending the previous message. This requires
knowledge only of past received relay symbols and therefore is possible to
implement for any finite relay encoding delay −l

◦Instantaneous relaying: Here the relay sends a function only of its current
received symbol. This is possible when the relay has access to the current
received symbol, which is the case for any l ≥0

◦Noncausal decode–forward: This scheme is possible only when the relay has
unlimited lookahead. The relay pre-decodes part or all of the message before
communication commences and cooperates with the sender to transmit the
message to the receiver

• Although instantaneous relaying alone can be optimal (e.g., the Sato and
AWGN RWD), a combination of decode–forward and instantaneous relaying
achieves higher rate in general

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)
Page 17 – 78


Key New Ideas and Techniques

• Cutset upper bound on capacity

• Coherent cooperation (similar to MAC with common message)

• Block Markov coding

• Use of multiple independent codebooks

• Decode–forward

• Backward decoding

• Use of binning in channel coding

• Compress–forward

• Amplify–forward

• Linear relaying

Appendix: Properties of C(P)

• The capacity of the AWGN-RC with average power constraints satisfies the
following:

(i) C(P) > 0 if P > 0 and approaches ∞as P →∞.

(ii) C(P) →0 as P →0.

(iii) C(P) is concave and strictly increasing in P .

• Proof:

(i) This follows from the fact that C(P) ≥C(g2P), the capacity of the direct
channel, which is strictly greater than zero for P > 0, and approaches infinity
as P →∞.

(ii) This follows from the fact that the cutset bound, which is greater than or
equal to C(P) approaches zero as P →0

LNIT: Relay Channels (2010-03-20 20:36)
Page 17 – 82


(iii) Concavity follows by the following “time-sharing” argument. For any
P, P ′ > 0 and ǫ > 0, there exists k and k′ such that C(P) < Ck(P) + ǫ and
C(P ′) < Ck′(P ′) + ǫ. Now, for any integers a, b, define α := ak/(ak + bk′),
where,

αC(P) + αC(P ′) ≤αCk(P) + αCk′(P ′) + ǫ

≤Cak+bk′ (αP + αP ′)) + ǫ

≤C (αP + αP ′)) + ǫ,

where the second inequality follows from the fact that αCk(P) + αCk′(P ′) is
achieved arbitrarily closely for some pmf p(xak+bk′) and some relaying
functions {˜x1j}ak+bk′
j=1
that is a mixture of the pmf p(xk) and relaying
functions {x1i}k
j=1 that achieve Ck(P) and the p(xk′) and {x′
1j}k′
j=1 that
achieve Ck′(P ′) (corresponding to “time-sharing”). Clearly, the set of pmfs
and functions achieved by “time-sharing” is a subset of the set of all possible
p(xak+bk′) and {x1i}ak+bk′
i=1
. Note that even though
Ck(P) = (1/k) maxp(xk),{x1i}k
j=1 I(Xk; Y k) may not be concave,
C(P) = supk Ck(P) is concave

That C(P) is strictly monotonically increasing in P follows from parts (i),
(ii), and concavity.

Appendix: Equivalence of Compress–Forward Lower Bound
Characterizations

• To show that the two characterizations are equal, denote the first
characterization by R1 and the second by R2

(a)
≤I(X, X1; Y ) −I(Y1; ˆY1|X1, Y ) + I( ˆY1; X|X1, Y )

= I(X, X1; Y ) + H( ˆY1|X1, X, Y1, Y ) −H( ˆY1|X1, X, Y )

= I(X, X1; Y ) −I(Y1; ˆY1|X, X1, Y ),

where (a) follows from the constraint in the expression of R2

Introduction

• So far, we have discussed one-way communication only. Many communication
systems are inherently two-way, allowing for cooperation through feedback and
interactive exchange of information between nodes

• In this lecture notes, we study the effect of feedback on the capacity of
single-hop channels and then present results on the two-way channel, which was
introduced by Shannon [1] as the first multiple-user network model

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 2


Capacity of DMC with Feedback

• Consider a DMC (X, p(y|x), Y) with noiseless causal feedback

M
ˆ
M

p(y|x)
Encoder
Decoder

Recall that the memoryless property is defined as p(yi|xi, yi−1) = pY |X(yi|xi)
for i ∈[1 : n]

• A (2nR, n) feedback code for the DMC consists of

1. a message set [1 : 2nR]

2. an encoder that assigns a symbol xi(m, yi−1) to each message m ∈[1 : 2nR]
and received sequence yi−1 ∈Yi−1 for i ∈[1 : n]

3. a decoder that assigns a message ˆm ∈[1 : 2nR] or an error message e to each
received sequence yn

• We assume that the message M is uniformly distributed over [1 : 2nR]

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 3

• Average probability of error, achievability, and capacity are defined as in the
nonfeedback case

• Shannon [2] showed that feedback does not increase the capacity of a DMC

• Theorem 1: The capacity of the DMC (X, p(y|x), Y) with noiseless causal
feedback is

CFB = C = max
p(x) I(X; Y )

• Proof: We only need to prove the converse. Note that by the memoryless
property, (M, Y i−1) →Xi →Yi form a Markov chain for all i ∈[1 : n]. Hence,
the proof of converse in Lecture Notes 3 holds for the DMC with feedback

• Nonetheless, feedback can help communication in several important ways

◦Feedback can greatly simplify coding and improve its performance [3, 4, 5]

◦Feedback can increase the capacity of channels with memory, e.g., [6]

◦Feedback can enlarge the capacity region of DM multiple user channels, e.g.,
[7, 8]

• Study of feedback also gives insights on the fundamental limit of two-way
communication

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 4


Iterative Refinement

• Consider a BEC with erasure probability p. Without feedback, we need to use
block error correcting codes to approach the channel capacity C = (1 −p)
bits/transmission. With feedback, however, we can achieve capacity by simply
retransmitting each bit immediately after it is erased. It can be shown that
roughly n = k/(1−p) transmissions suffice to send k bits of information reliably.
Thus, with feedback there is no need for sophisticated error correcting codes

• This simple observation can be extended to other channels with feedback. The
basic idea is to first send a message at a higher rate than channel capacity, and
then to iteratively refine the receiver’s knowledge about the message

• We discuss this general paradigm of feedback communication through three key
examples

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 5

Schalkwijk–Kailath Coding for the AWGN Channel

• Consider an AWGN channel with noiseless causal feedback, where the channel
output at time i is Yi = Xi + Zi, and the noise {Zi} is a WGN(1) process.

We present a simple coding scheme by Schalkwijk and Kailath [3, 4] that

noise Z0 = Y0 −X0

previous transmission

◦Consider the channel with input X1 and output ˆX1(Y n) = E(X1|Y n).
Because the MMSE estimate ˆX1(Y n) is a linear function of X1 and Zn with
I(X1; ˆX1) = n C(P) for Gaussian X1, the channel from ˆX1 to X1 is
equivalent to an AWGN channel with effective SNR 22n C(P ) −1 (independent
of the specific input distribution on X1). Hence the Schalkwijk–Kailath
coding transforms n uses of the AWGN channel with SNR P into a single use

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 10


of the channel with SNR 22n C(P ) −1. Thus to achieve the capacity, the
sender can transmit X1 = θ(m) directly without any initial transmission and
iterations on Z0

◦Another implication of the linearity of the Schalkwijk–Kaliath coding is that it
can be used as is when the additive noise is not Gaussian. In this case, by
Chebychev’s inequality, P (n)

Horstein Coding for the BSC

• Consider a BSC(p), where the channel output at time i is Yi = Xi ⊕Zi and the

x1 = φ(θ0, f0)

• The Horstein coding is a special case of a more general posterior matching

Block Feedback Coding for the BSC

• In the previous two coding schemes, the encoder sends a large (undecodable)
amount of uncoded information in the initial few transmissions and then
iteratively refines the receiver’s knowledge in subsequent transmissions

Here we present a coding scheme that implements iterative refinement at the
block level rather than the transmission level. In this coding scheme, the
encoder initially transmits an uncoded block of information and then refines the
receiver’s knowledge of it in subsequent blocks

• Again consider a BSC(p), where the channel output at time i is Yi = Xi ⊕Zi
and the noise {Zi} is a Bern(p) process

• Codebook generation and encoding:

◦To send message m ∈[1 : 2n], the encoder first transmits the binary
representation X1 = Xn(m) of the message m

◦Upon receiving the feedback signal Y1, the encoder compresses
Z1 = X1 ⊕Y1 losslessly and transmits the index X2(Z1) over the next
n(H(p) + δ(ǫ)) transmissions

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 17

◦Upon receiving the resulting feedback signal Y2, the encoder transmits the
corresponding noise index X3(Z2) over the next n(H(p) + δ(ǫ))2

transmissions

◦Continuing in the same manner, in the j-th block, the encoder transmits the
noise index Xj(Zj−1) over n(H(p) + δ(ǫ))j−1 transmissions

◦After k1 such unequal-length block transmissions, the encoder transmits the
index of Zk1 over n(H(p) + δ(ǫ))k1−1 · k2 transmissions using repetition
coding (i.e., transmits the noise index k2 times without any coding)

◦The rate of this code is 1/(�k1
j=1(H(p) + δ(ǫ))j−1 + (H(p) + δ(ǫ))k1 · k2)

• Decoding and the analysis of the probability of error: Decoding is performed
backwards. The decoder first finds Zk1 (by the majority decoding rule), then
Zk1−1 through X(Zk1−1) = Yk1 ⊕Zk1, then Zk1−2, and so on, until Z1 is
decoded. The message is finally decoded through X1(m) = Y1 ⊕Z1
By choosing k1 = log(n/ log n) and k2 = log2 n, it can be shown (check!) that
the probability of error over all transmission blocks →0 while the achieved rate
approaches 1/(�∞
j=1(H(p) + δ(δ))j−1) = 1 −H(p) −δ(ǫ) as n →∞

• This simple coding scheme is originally due to Weldon [16] and has been
extended to arbitrary DMCs [17, 5]

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 18

for j = 1, 2 and i ∈[1 : n], and a decoder ˆm(yn). The probability of error,
achievability, and capacity region are defined as before

• Feedback can enlarge the capacity region (by creating cooperation between

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 19

• Capacity region with feedback is not known in general

• Capacity region with feedback is known for 2-sender AWGN-MAC [8]

• Cooperative outer bound: Any achievable rate pair (R1, R2) for the DM-MAC
with feedback must satisfy the conditions

R1 ≤I(X1; Y |X2),

R2 ≤I(X2; Y |X1),

R1 + R2 ≤I(X1, X2; Y )

for some p(x1, x2)

To prove this outer bound, note that by the memoryless property,
(M1, M2, Y i−1) →(X1i, X2i) →Yi form a Markov chain. Hence, as in the
proof of converse for the DM-MAC without feedback in Lecture Notes 4, the
following conditions continue to hold

R1 ≤I(X1Q; YQ|X2Q, Q),

R2 ≤I(X2Q; YQ|X1Q, Q),

R1 + R2 ≤I(X1Q, X2Q; YQ, Q)

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 20


Now, since Q →(X1Q, X2Q) →YQ form a Markov chain, the above inequalities
can be further relaxed to

R1 ≤I(X1Q; YQ|X2Q),

R2 ≤I(X2Q; YQ|X1Q),

R1 + R2 ≤I(X1Q, X2Q; YQ)

Identifying X1 := X1Q, X2 := X2Q, and Y := YQ completes the proof

Note that unlike the nonfeedback case X1 and X2 are no longer conditionally
independent given Q. Hence, the outer bound is evaluated over all joint pmfs
p(x1, x2)

• Later we find constraints on the set of joint pmfs that can be induced between
X1 and X2 through feedback, which yield a tighter outer bound in general

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 21

Cover–Leung Inner Bound

• Theorem 2 (Cover–Leung Inner Bound) [18]: A rate pair (R1, R2) is achievable
for a DM-MAC with feedback if it satisfies the conditions
R1 < I(X1; Y |X2, U),

R2 < I(X2; Y |X1, U),

R1 + R2 < I(X1, X2; Y )
for some p(u)p(x1|u)p(x2|u), where |U| ≤min{|X1| · |X2| + 1, |Y| + 2}

• This region is convex and has a very similar form to the nonfeedback capacity
region given by the set of (R1, R2) pairs satisfying
R1 ≤I(X1; Y |X2, Q),

R2 ≤I(X2; Y |X1, Q),

R1 + R2 ≤I(X1, X2; Y |Q)
for some p(q)p(x1|q)p(x2|q), where |Q| ≤2. In fact, the only difference comes
from the conditioning in the sum-rate bound

• This region was shown to be optimal for the class of DM-MAC for which X1 is
a deterministic function of (X2, Y ) [19]. However, it is not optimal in general

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 22


Example: Binary Erasure MAC

• Consider the binary erasure DM-MAC discussed in Lecture Notes 4, in which the
channel input symbols X1, X2 are binary and the channel output symbol
Y = X1 + X2 is ternary

• We know that the capacity region without feedback is given by

R1 ≤1,

R2 ≤1,

R1 + R2 ≤3/2

• First we show that this region can be achieved by a simple feedback coding
scheme reminiscent of Weldon’s coding. We focus on the symmetric rate pair
R1 = R2 = R

Suppose each encoder sends k independent bits uncoded. Then roughly k/2
bits are erased (i.e., Y = 0 + 1 = 1 + 0 = 1 is received). Since encoder 1 knows
the exact locations of the erasures through feedback, it can retransmit the
erased bits over the next k/2 transmissions (while encoder 2 sends X2 = 0).
The decoder now can decode both messages. Since k bits are sent over k + k/2
transmissions, the rate R = 2/3 is achievable

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 23

Since encoder 2 also knows the k/2 erased bits for encoder 1 through feedback,
the two encoders can cooperate by each sending half of the k/2 erased bits over
the following k/4 transmissions. These retransmssions result in roughly k/8
erased bits, which can be retransmitted again over the following k/16
transmissions, and so on. Proceeding recursively, we can achieve a rate
arbitrarily close to k/(k + k/4 + k/16 + · · · ) = 3/4

• The above coding scheme can be improved by inducing further cooperation
between the encoders [7]. Again suppose k independent bits are sent initially.
Since both encoders know the k/2 erased bits, they can cooperate to send them
over the next (k/2)/ log 3 transmissions. Hence we can achieve the rate
R = k/(k + k/ log 9) = 0.7602 > 3/4 !

• Now evaluating the Cover–Leung inner bound with U ∼Bern(1/2) and
Xj = U ⊕Vj, j = 1, 2, where Vj ∼Bern(0.2377) and U, V1, V2 are independent
of each other, we can achieve R = 0.7911

This rate can be shown to be optimal [19]

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 24


Proof of Achievability [20]

• The proof of achievability involves superposition coding, block Markov coding,
and backward decoding

• We consider b blocks, each consisting of n transmissions

A sequence of b −1 i.i.d. message pairs (M1j, M2j) ∈[1 : 2nR1] × [1 : 2nR2],
j ∈[1 : b −1], is sent over nb transmissions

At the end of block j −1, sender 1 decodes M2,j−1 and then in block j both
senders cooperatively transmit information to the receiver (carried by U) to
resolve the remaining uncertainty about M2,j−1, superimposed with information
about the new messages (M1j, M2j)

• Codebook: As in the Schalkwijk–Kailath coding for the AWGN channel with

◦With feedback it can be shown that (R1, R2) = (1, 1) is achievable:

Suppose we want to send the binary sequences xn
1 and xn
2 to receivers Y1 and
Y2, respectively

Begin by sending (0, x11, x21) over the channel

Using the feedback link, the sender can determine the outcome z1 of the
random variable Z1
The triple (z1, x12, x22) is then sent over the channel

Upon receiving the common information z1, the decoders recover x11 and x21
Thus using the feedback link, the sender and receivers can recover zn and
therefore each receiver can recover its intended sequence perfectly, and
(R1, R2) = (1, 1) is achievable with feedback

As shown in this example, feedback can increase the capacity by letting the
encoder broadcast common channel information to all decoders

• The capacity of the DM-TWC is not known in general

The difficulty arises from the fact that two information flows share the same
channel, inflicting interference to each other. In addition, each node has to play
two competing roles of communicating its own message and providing feedback
to help the other node

Simple Inner and Outer Bounds on the Capacity Region

• Shannon’s Inner Bound [2]: A rate pair (R1, R2) is achievable for a DM-TWC
(X1 × X2, p(y1, y2|x1, x2), Y1 × Y2) if
R1 < I(X1; Y2|X2, Q), R2 < I(X2; Y1|X1, Q)
for some p(q)p(x1|q)p(x2|q)

◦The proof follows by the usual achievability argument using random codebook
generation, joint typicality decoding, and the packing lemma. The encoders
completely ignore the received outputs (no feedback)

◦This inner bound is tight when the channel is decoupled into two separate
DMCs, that is, p(y1, y2|x1, x2) = p(y1|x2)p(y2|x1)

◦The bound is not tight in general, as shown by a counterexample [26]

• Shannon’s Outer Bound [2]: Any achievable rate pair (R1, R2) for a DM-TWC
(X1 × X2, p(y1, y2|x1, x2), Y1 × Y2) must satisfy the conditions
R1 ≤I(X1; Y2|X2), R2 ≤I(X2; Y1|X1)
for some p(x1, x2)

◦This outer bound can be easily established using standard weak converse
techniques

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 48


◦This outer bound is a special case of the cutset outer bound that will be
discussed in Lecture Notes 19 and is not tight in general

• Example: Consider the binary multiplier channel (BMC) defined by
X1 = X2 = {0, 1} and Y1 = Y2 = Y = X1 · X2

X1 ∈{0, 1}
X2 ∈{0, 1}

Y ∈{0, 1}
Y ∈{0, 1}

◦The Shannon inner bound is the set of rate pairs such that

R1 < p2H(p1),

R2 < p1H(p2)

for some 1/2 ≤p1, p2 ≤1. We set pX1(1) = p1 and pX2(1) = p2

◦The Shannon outer bound is the set of rate pairs such that

R1 ≤(1 −p1)H

Page 18 – 49

pX1,X2(0, 1) = p2, pX1,X2(1, 1) = 1 −p1 −p2

Dependence Balance Outer Bound

• Consider the DM-TWC with common output Y1 = Y2 = Y

• Theorem 6 [27]: If a rate pair (R1, R2) is achievable for the DM-TWC with
common output (X1 × X2, p(y|x1, x2), Y), then it must satisfy the conditions

R1 ≤I(X1; Y |X2, U),

R2 ≤I(X2; Y |X1, U)

for some p(u, x1, x2) such that

I(X1; X2|U) ≤I(X1; X2|Y, U)

and |U| ≤3

• The proof follows (check!) from standard weak converse techniques by
identifying Ui = Y i−1

• The dependence balance outer bound is identical to Shannon’s outer bound,
except for the extra dependence balance condition
I(X1; X2|U) ≤I(X1; X2|Y, U). This condition limits the set of possible joint
pmfs that can be formed through sequential transmissions

• This bound is not tight in general

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 51

• For the case of the BMC, the dependence balance bound gives the same upper
bound on the symmetric capacity. However, an ingenuous modification [27] of
the technique leads to the tighter upper bound C ≤0.6463

• A similar outer bound can be derived for the DM-MAC with feedback. In this
case, we add the inequality

R1 + R2 ≤I(X1, X2; Y |U)

to those already in the DM-TWC case. This outer bound is in general tighter
than the cooperative outer bound for the DM-MAC with feedback, due to the
dependence balance condition on the set of joint pmfs on (X1, X2)

LNIT: Interactive Communication (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 18 – 52

Key New Ideas and Techniques

• Feedback does not increase the capacity of a DMC

• Iterative refinement

• Feedback can increase capacity of DM multiple user channels

• Directed information

• Open problems:

◦What is the feedback capacity region of the AWGN-MAC with more than 2
senders?

◦What is the feedback capacity region of the AWGN BC? (Why does feedback
increase capacity here?)

Discrete Memoryless Multicast Network

• An N-node discrete memoryless multicast network (DM-MN)
(X1 × · · · × XN, p(y2, . . . , yN|x1, . . . , xN), Y2 × · · · × YN) consists of a source
alphabet X1, N −1 sender–receiver alphabet pairs (Xj, Yj), j ∈[2 : N], and a
collection of conditional pmfs p(y2, . . . , yN|x1, . . . , xN)

Source node 1 wishes to send a message M to a set D ⊆[2 : N] of destination
nodes

p(y2, . . . , yN|x1, x2, . . . , xN)
X1
M

(XN, YN)
ˆ
MN

(X2, Y2)

(Xj, Yj)

ˆ
Mj

LNIT: Discrete Memoryless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 19 – 2


• Formally, a (2nR, n) code for a DM-MN consists of:

1. A message set [1 : 2nR]

2. A source encoder that assigns a codeword xn
1(m) to each message
m ∈[1 : 2nR]

3. A set of N −1 relay encoders: Encoder k assigns a symbol xki(yi−1
k
) to
every received sequence yi−1
k
for i ∈[1 : n]

4. A set of decoders: Decoder k, k ∈D, assigns ˆmk to each yn
k

• M is uniformly distributed over [1 : 2nR]

• The probability of error is P (n)
e
= P
�
ˆ
Mk ̸= M for some k ∈D
�

• A rate R is said to be achievable if there exists a sequence of (2nR, n) codes
with P (n)
e
→0 as n →∞

• The capacity of the DM-MN is the supremum of the set of achievable rates

LNIT: Discrete Memoryless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 19 – 3

• Examples:

◦If D = [2 : N] and p(y2, . . . , yN|x1, . . . , xN) = p(y2, . . . , yN|x1), then this is
a DM-BC with common message only

◦If N = 3, X3 = ∅, and D = {3}, then this is a DM-RC

◦If D = {N}, then we denote the network as a discrete memoryless relay
network (DM-RN)

• There is no single-letter (i.e., computable) characterization of the capacity of a
general DM-MN (recall that the relay channel is a special case of the multicast
network)

LNIT: Discrete Memoryless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 19 – 4

Decode–Forward Lower Bound

• Theorem 2 (Decode–Forward Lower Bound) [2, 3]: The capacity of the DM-MN
p(y2, . . . , yN|xN) with any set D ⊆[2 : N] of destination nodes is lower

• The decode–forward lower bound is also tight for the relay channel

• The decode–forward lower bound holds for any set D ⊆[2 : N] of destination
nodes

• The bound can be further improved by removing some relay nodes or reordering
nodes and taking the best subset/permutation

Sliding Window Decoding

• Achievability of the multicast decode–forward lower bound involves the new idea
of sliding-window decoding [4, 5]

• We first revisit the single-relay case as an example and prove the
decode–forward lower bound using an alternative decoding scheme to backward
decoding in Lecture Notes 17

• As usual, we consider b transmission blocks, each consisting of of n
transmissions and use a block Markov coding scheme

A sequence of b −1 i.i.d. messages, mj ∈[1 : 2nR], j ∈[1 : b −1], is to be sent
over the channel in nb transmissions

• Codebook generation: We use the same codebook generation as in cooperative
multi-hop in Lecture Notes 17

Noisy Network Coding Lower Bound

• We generalize the network coding theorem to obtain the following

Theorem 3 (Noisy Network Coding Lower Bound) [6]: The capacity of the
DM-MN p(y2, . . . , yN|xN) is lower bounded by
C ≥
max
�N
k=1 p(xk)p(ˆyk|yk,xk)
min
k∈D
min
S⊆[1:N]
1∈S,k∈Sc

�
I(X(S); ˆY (Sc), Yk|X(Sc))

−I(Y (S); ˆY (S)|XN, ˆY (Sc), Yk)
�
,

where the maximum is taken over all pmfs �N
k=1 p(xk)p(ˆyk|yk, xk) and
Y1 = ˆY1 = ∅by convention

• The noisy network coding lower bound can be compared to the cutset upper
bound:

◦the first term is similar to the cutset bound with Y (Sc) replaced by the
“compressed version” ˆY (Sc),

◦the second term is subtracted, and

◦the maximization is over independent XN (no coherent cooperation)

• Note that this lower bound reduces to the compress–forward lower bound for the
relay channel [7, 8], which was discussed in Lecture Notes 17

Deterministic Multicast Network

• Consider the class of deterministic multicast networks where the received symbol
Yk at each node k ∈[2 : N] is a deterministic function of transmitted symbols,
i.e., Yk = yk(XN)

• The cutset upper bound simplifies to

C ≤max
p(xN) min
k∈D
min
S:1∈S,k∈Sc H(Y (Sc)|X(Sc))

• On the other hand, by taking ˆYk = Yk for all k ∈[2 : N], the noisy network
coding lower bound reduces to the following lower bound [9]:

C ≥
max
�N
k=1 p(xk)
min
k∈D
min
S:1∈S,k∈Sc H(Y (Sc)|X(Sc))

where the maximization is over product pmfs instead of arbitrary joint pmf as in
the cutset bound

• In general, these two bounds do not coincide. But there are several interesting
special cases where they coincide

LNIT: Discrete Memoryless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 19 – 19

• Examples

◦Noiseless multicast network: A noiseless multicast network (N, E, C) with
destinations D in Lecture Notes 16 is a special case of deterministic multicast
networks with Yk = X(Nk), where Nk = {j : (j, k) ∈E} for k ∈[2 : N].
Each link (j, k) ∈E carries an input symbol xjk ∈Xjk with link capacity
Cjk = log |Xjk|

In this case, the capacity is given by the max-flow min-cut theorem, which
coincides with the cutset bound achieved by the uniform pmf on all input
symbols

◦Deterministic multicast network with no interference [2, 10]: Suppose each
node receives a collection of single-variable functions of input symbols, i.e.,
Yk = (yk1(X1), . . . , ykN(XN)), k ∈[2 : N]. Then the cutset bound is
achieved by a product input pmf and the capacity is

C =
max
�N
k=1 p(xk)
min
k∈D
min
S:1∈S,k∈Sc
�

j∈S
H({Ylj : l ∈Sc})

For example, if N = 3, D = {2, 3},
Y2 = (y21(X1), y23(X3)), Y3 = (y31(X1), y32(X2)), then

C =
max
p(x1)p(x2)p(x3) min{H(Y21, Y31), H(Y21) + H(Y23), H(Y31) + H(Y32)}

LNIT: Discrete Memoryless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 19 – 20

by the uniform product input pmf [9] and

C = min
k∈D

S:1∈S,k∈Sc rank(G(S)) log q,

where G(S) is defined such that

Y (Sc) = G(S)X(S) + G′(S)X(Sc)

◦Deterministic relay channel: Suppose the network has three nodes with
output symbols
Y2 = y2(X1, X2), Y3 = y3(X1, X2)
Then the capacity is

C = max
p(x1,x2) min{H(Y3), H(Y2, Y3|X2)},

which is achieved by partial decode-and-forward (cf. Lecture Notes 17). In
this case, the compress–forward lower bound is not tight in general

Message Repetition Compress–Forward Coding

• Achievability of the noisy network coding lower bound involves the new idea of
message repetition compress–forward coding. Unlike other block Markov coding
techniques we have seen so far, the message repetition coding sends the same
message m ∈[1 : 2nbR] over b blocks (nb transmissions), while the relays send
compressed versions of the channel observation of the previous block.
Equivalently, the encoder transmits a long codeword of length nb, while relays
compress and forward observations of length n (over b blocks)

• We first consider the single-relay case as an example and then develop the main
idea into the general DM-MN subsequently

• Consider the DM-RC p(y2, y3|x1, x2) with source node 1, relay node 2, and
destination node 3. By convention Y1 = X3 = ∅. We prove the achievability of
the compress–forward lower bound:

C ≥
max
p(x1)p(x2)p(ˆy2|y2,x2) min{I(X1, X2; Y3)−I(Y2; ˆY2|X1, X2, Y3), I(X1; ˆY2, Y3|X2)}

LNIT: Discrete Memoryless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 19 – 22

Discrete Memoryless Multiple Source Network

• An N-node discrete memoryless network (DMN)
(X1 × · · · × XN, p(yN|xN), Y1 × · · · × YN) consists of N sender–receiver
alphabet pairs (Xk, Yk), k ∈[1 : N], and a collection of conditional pmfs p(yN|xN)

Each node k ∈[1 : N] wishes to send a message Mk to a set Dk ⊆[1 : N]\{k}
of destination nodes

• Formally, a (2nR1, 2nR2, . . . , 2nRN, n) code for a DMN consists of:

1. Message sets [1 : 2nR1], . . . , [1 : 2nRN−1]

2. A set of N encoders: Encoder k assigns a symbol xki(mk, yi−1

Note that each node has only one message to send, but can be a destination
for zero, one, or more messages. A node can also serve as a relay for
messages from other nodes

• Assume that the message tuple (M1, . . . , MN) is uniformly distributed over
[1 : 2nR1] × · · · × [1 : 2nRN]

• The capacity region of the DMN is the closure of the set of achievable rates

• Note that this model includes the DM-MAC, DM-IC, DM-RC, and the
DM-TWC as special cases

However, it does not include broadcast networks with multiple messages sent
from a single source. For this case, the outer bound we derive will apply only to
the sum rates from each sender

• Theorem 4 (Cutset Outer Bound): If a rate tuple (R1, . . . , RN) is achievable,
then for some p(x1, . . . , xN),

• The outer bound is not tight in general. Sometimes we can obtain tighter, single
letter bounds using tricks (e.g., auxiliary random variables, EPI, Mrs. Gerber’s
lemma) specific to each channel model

• Examples where the bound is tight:

◦Noiseless networks with the same set of destinations (D1 = · · · DN = D) (cf.
Lecture Notes 16)

◦Physically degraded and classes of deterministic relay networks [1, 2, 9]

sender–receiver pair interference channel

deterministic networks with no interference, and linear finite-field deterministic
networks), then the network compress–forward coding scheme achieves the
capacity region

AWGN Network Model

• Motivated by wireless networks, we consider the AWGN network: At time i,

Y N(i) = GXN(i) + ZN(i),

where Yj(i), j ∈[1 : N], is the reception at node j, G ∈RN×N is the channel
gain matrix, Xj(i), j ∈[1 : N], is the transmission at node j, and {ZN(i)} is a
vector of independent WGN(1) processes

• We assume average power constraint P on each sender:
n

j (i)) ≤nP for all j ∈[1 : N]

• Given the message requirement (e.g., relay, multicast, multiple unicast), the
definitions of codes, probability of error, achievability, and capacity region follow
the corresponding DM network

• We consider two channel gain models

◦Constant network: G is an arbitrary constant gain matrix

◦Random network: G is a random gain matrix that depends on the random
placement of nodes in the unit area. The path gain Gjk between two nodes j

and k with distance r is often modeled by g(r) = r−ν/2 for some ν > 2
(power law) or more generally by g(r) = e−γr/2r−ν/2 for some γ ≥0 and
ν > 2 (power law with absorption)

• The cutset bounds in Lecture Notes 19 can be easily specialized to the Gaussian
network model. For example, if (R1, . . . , RN) is achievable for the multi-source
Gaussian network with destination sets (D1, . . . , DN), then for some

• In general, it is extremely hard to characterize the capacity region in a
computable (i.e., single-letter) form, even for simple networks (e.g., relay
channel or interference channel). Can we still learn and say something about
information flow in a general Gaussian network? We discuss two approaches:

◦Bounds within constant gap: Here we derive bounds on capacity that differ by
no more than some constant (cf. the example in Lecture Notes 6 regarding
the AWGN-IC capacity within 1/2-bit). Such bounds provide guarantees on
the rates of the coding schemes that achieve the inner bounds

◦Scaling laws: Here we investigate how capacity scales with the number of
nodes in the network. This approach can shed light on optimal network
architecture designs and protocols

Page 20 – 4

Capacity Scaling Laws

• We begin with some simple examples where the set S of message source nodes
and the set D of message destination nodes are disjoint, i.e., Rj = 0, j ∈S and
S ∩D = ∅, and the rest of the nodes act as relays to help other nodes
communicate their messages

• We then consider a random network model, where the source and destination
nodes themselves can also act as relays

• We only consider network symmetric capacity C(N), which is the supremum of
the set of symmetirc rates R such that the rate tuple (R, . . . , R) is achievable

◦In few scenarios we know C(N) exactly

In other scenarios we only know its order, i.e., how it scales with N

But in more general scenarios, we have only loose upper and lower bounds on
its order

LNIT: Gaussian Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 20 – 8


• Questions we wish to answer using scaling laws:

◦How bad is interference?

◦How does path loss affect network symmetric capacity?

◦How bad is direct communication, i.e., when no cooperation via feedback or
relaying is allowed?

◦How useful is relaying?

◦What architectures (transmission schemes) are good for large wireless
networks?

LNIT: Gaussian Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 20 – 9

Achievable Rates

• Capacity of the AWGN relay channel is not known, even for N = 1

• How does C(N) scale with N?

• Without relaying, the maximum rate from the source to the destination is
C (PN −ν) = Ω(N −ν)

• Consider a simple multi-hop scheme, where signals are Gaussian and interference
is treated as noise (cf. Lecture Notes 17). In each block:

◦The source transmits a new message to the first relay

◦Relay j −1 transmits its most recently received message to relay j

◦Relay N sends its most recently received message to destination

• The interference power at receiver j is: Ij =

N
�

k=0

|k −j|−νP

• Thus C(N) ≥minj C (P/(Ij + 1))

• Since ν > 1, Ij = O(1) for all j
Thus C(N) = Ω(1)—a huge increase from rate without relaying

Gupta–Kumar Random Network Approach

• We know that finding capacity for specific networks when broadcasting,
interference, and/or relaying are included is in general quite intractable

• The Gupta–Kumar random network approach aims to establish scaling laws for
capacity that apply to most large networks

• The results can help us better understand the roles of interference and
cooperation in large networks, which could lead to better wireless network
architectures

LNIT: Gaussian Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 20 – 18

as a relay to help other nodes communicate their messages

We assume the same average power constraint P on each sender

• Source j wishes to send a message Mj ∈[1 : 2nRj] reliably to destination
j + N. The messages are assumed to be independent

• We wish to determine the scaling law for the symmetric capacity C(N) that
holds with high probability (w.h.p.), i.e., with probability ≥(1 −ǫN), where
ǫN →0 as N →∞(thus holds for most large networks generated in this
random manner)

◦They assumed signal-to-interference SIR and protocol models for successful
transmission

◦They roughly showed that the symmetric capacity under these models scales
as Θ

Cells of size log N
Active cells

• We assume the same rate for all S-D pairs

• A block Markov transmission scheme is used, where each source sends messages
over several transmission blocks

• The cells are divided into 9 groups as shown in the figure, and each transmission
block is divided into 9 cell-blocks

LNIT: Gaussian Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 20 – 23

• A cell is said to be active if its nodes are allowed to transmit

• Each cell is active only during one out the 9 cell-blocks. Nodes in non-active
cells act as receivers

• Each message is sent from a source to its destination using other nodes in cells
along the straight line joining them (referred to as an S-D line) as relays

S

S-D lines

• Transmission from each node in an active cell to nodes in its 4 neighboring cells
is performed using Gaussian random codes with power P and each receiver
treats interference from other senders as noise

LNIT: Gaussian Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 20 – 24


• Let S(N) be the maximum number of sources in a cell and L(N) be the
maximum number of S-D lines passing through a cell, over all cells

• Each cell-block is divided into S(N) + L(N) node-blocks for time-division
transmission by nodes inside each active cell

◦Each source node in an active cell broadcasts a new message during its
node-block using Gaussian random codes code with power P

◦One of the nodes in the active cell acts as a relay for the S-D pairs that send
their messages through this cell. It relays the messages during the alloted
L(N) node-blocks using optimal AWGN channel codes with power P

Analysis of the Probability of Failure

• The cellular TDM scheme we described fails if

1. There is a cell with no nodes in it. Denote this event by E1

2. A transmission from a node in a cell to a node in a neighboring cell fails.
Denote this event by E2

• The total probability that the scheme fails is

P(E) = P(E1) + P(E2 ∩Ec
1)

We bound each term as follows:

• It straightforward to show that P(E1) →0 as N →∞

• Consider the event (E2 ∩Ec
1) :

◦From the cell geometry, the distance between each transmitting node in a cell
and each receiving node in its neighboring cells is always ≤(5 log N)1/2

◦Since each transmitting node uses power P, the received power at a node in a
neighboring cell is always ≥(5 log N)−ν/2P

Key New Ideas and Techniques

• Network sum-capacity C(N) for AWGN networks:

◦We know it exactly for the N-sender MAC

◦We know its order for the N-S-D pair interference network example when
a = 1

◦We know its order for the N-relay network example

• Random network model (find order capacity for most networks)

• Open problem: How does C(N) grow with N for the symmetric N-S-D pair
interference network with a ̸= 1 ?

LNIT: Gaussian Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 20 – 35

Appendix: Proof of Lemma 1

• It is straightforward to show that the number of sources in each cell
S(N) = O(log N) w.h.p.

• We now bound L(N). Consider a torus with the same area and the same square
cell division as the square. For each S-D pair on the torus, send each packet
along 4 possible lines connecting them. Clearly for every configuration of nodes,
each cell in the torus has at least as many S-D lines crossing it as in the original
square. The reason for considering the torus is that the pmf of the number of
lines in each cell is the same

Let Hj be the total number of hops taken by packets travelling along one of 4
lines between S-D pair j, j ∈[1 : N]. It is not difficult to see that the expected
length of each path is Θ(N 1/2). Since the hops are along cells having
side-length (log N)1/2,

E(Hj) = Θ
�
(N/ log N)1/2�

a line between S-D pair j passes through cell c, i.e.,

0
otherwise

for j ∈[1 : N] and c ∈[1 : N/ log N]

Introduction

• In the noiseless networks lecture notes, we assumed that the sources are
independent and to be recovered losslessly, and the network is a directed acyclic
graph

• In this lecture notes, we generalize this setting in two ways:

◦We first consider single-source multiple descriptions over networks modeled by
directed acyclic graphs, and

◦Then, we consider correlated sources over networks with two-way
communication and broadcasting

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 2


Multiple Descriptions Network

• Consider an N-node communication network modeled by a directed acyclic
graph G = (N, E). Source node 1 observes a DMS X and each node j ∈[2 : N]
wishes to estimate X with a prescribed distortion Dj under a distortion measure
dj(x, ˆxj). What are the link rates required to achieve the prescribed distortions?

1

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 3

• A ({2nRjk}(j,k)∈E, n) code for the multiple description network G = (N, E)
consists of

1. A source encoder that assigns an index m1j(xn) ∈[1 : 2nR1j] to each
xn ∈X n

2. A set of N −1 relay encoders: For each (k, l) ∈E, encoder k assigns an
index mkl ∈[1 : 2nRkl] to every received index tuple {mjk : (j, k) ∈E}

3. A set of N −1 decoders: Decoder k assigns an estimate ˆxn
j to every received
index tuple {mjk : (j, k) ∈E}

• A rate–distortion tuple ({Rjk}(j,k)∈E, D2, . . . , DN) is achievable if there exists a
sequence of ({2nRjk}, n) codes such that

lim sup
n→∞E(dj(Xn, ˆXn
j )) ≤Dj, j ∈[2 : N]

• The problem is to find the rate–distortion region R(D2, . . . , DN) for a multiple
description network (N, E), which is the closure of the set of achievable rate
tuples {Rjk}(j,k)∈E such that ({Rjk}(j,k)∈E, D2, . . . , DN) is achievable

• Note that without loss of generality we only need to consider the case where the
rate on each outgoing edge is less than or equal to the sum of the rates on the
incoming edges at its starting node (why?)

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 4


• It is easy to show the following cutset outer bound on the rate–distortion region:
if ({Rjk}(j,k)∈E, D2, . . . , DN) is achievable, then for some p(ˆxN
2 |x)
�

j∈S,k∈Sc
Rjk ≥I(X; ˆX(Sc)) for all S ⊆[1 : N],

where ˆX(S) is the ordered vector of ˆXk, k ∈S

• If dj, j ∈[2 : N], is a Hamming distortion and Dj = 0 or 1, then the problem
reduces to the lossless coding over the noiseless multicast network with the set
of destination nodes D = {j ∈[2 : N] : Dj = 0}. The rate–distortion region is
the set of rate tuples such that the resulting min-cut capacity C (minized over
all destination nodes) satisfies H(X) ≤C

• The rate–distortion region for a multiple description network is not known in
general. In the following, we consider a few nontrivial special cases

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 5

Special Cases

• Tree network: If the network is a tree, i.e., there is only one incoming edge to
each node, then the rate–distortion region is known

Theorem 1: The rate–distortion region for distortion tuple (D2, D3, . . . , DN) is
the set of rate tuples {Rjk : (j, k) ∈E} satisfying

Rjk ≥I(X; ˆX(Sc
k)),

where Sc
k consists of k and the set of nodes that precedes it (i.e., if node
l ∈Sc
k, then node k is on the path from node 1 to node l)

◦It suffices to consider p(ˆxN

• The multiple description network can be generalized by assuming correlated
sources, allowing two-way communication, and/or allowing nodes to broadcast
to subsets of other nodes, i.e., modeling the network by a hypergraph. Such
generalizations include all the single-hop multiple user source coding problems
we have discussed so far, as well as the interactive source coding problems we
discuss in the following

of the other node. What are the set of necessary and sufficient communication

• The problem is to find the rate region, which is the closure of the set of

• The solution to this problem is quite straightforward. By the Slepian-Wolf

in two rounds of communication. It is also straightforward to show that this set
of rate pairs is necessary

• Remark: For error-free communication, it can be shown that R1 ≥H(X1) and
R2 ≥H(X2) are necessary in some cases, e.g., when p(x, y) > 0 for all (x, y)
pairs [2]

Multi-Way Lossless Source Coding

• Now, consider a more general setting of a noiseless broadcast network with
N-nodes and N-DMS (X1 × · · · × XN, p(x1, . . . , xN)). Node j ∈[1 : N]
observes source Xj, and the nodes communicate over a noiseless broadcast
channel such that all the nodes in some subset A ⊆[1 : N] can losslessly
decode all sources. This problem is also known as communication for
omniscience (CFO) [3]

• Without loss of generality, assume that the nodes communicate in a round robin
fashion in q rounds, where q ∈Z+ is divisible by N and may depend on the
code block length, such that node j ∈[1 : N] broadcasts in rounds
j, N + j, . . . , q −N + j. Note that any other node communication order can be
achieved using this “protocol” by appropriately having nodes not communicate
(or send an agreed upon message) during some of their allotted rounds

• A (2nr1, 2nr2, . . . , 2nrq, n) code for the CFO problem consists of:

1. N encoders, one for each node j ∈[1 : N]. At round
lj ∈{j, N + j, . . . , q −N + j}, encoder j sends an index
mlj(xn
j , mlj−1) ∈[1 : 2
nrlj], i.e., a function of its source vector and all
previously transmitted indices

• Proof of converse: First assume that A = {N}, i.e., only node N wishes to
losslessly estimate the N-DMS. Consider the following cutset argument:

◦Partition the nodes of the network into two sets S ⊆{1, 2, . . . , N −1} and
Sc, the latter of which by definition contains the receiver node N

◦Imagine the nodes on each side of the “cut” to be grouped into a single
“super-node”. Super-node S has the sources X(S) and super-node Sc has
the sources X(Sc)

What is the minimum number of bits that must flow from super-node S to
super-node Sc in order for the receiver to know all the sources?

◦Clearly, a lower bound on the amount of information that must flow from S
to Sc is �

j∈S Rj ≥H(X(S)|X(Sc))

◦By considering all such node partitions, we obtain an outer bound to the
optimal rate region that coincides with the Slepian–Wolf region

The converse follows by applying the above cutset bound for each node k ∈A
as a receiver

• Remark: It is indeed surprising that the above simple cutset bound is achievable
in this setup and that interactive communication does not enlarge the rate region

Two-Way Lossy Source Coding

• Let (X1, X2) be a 2-DMS and d1, d2 be two distortion functions. Assume a
2-node communication network in which node 1 observes X1 and node 2
observes X2. The nodes communicate over a two-way noiseless channel so that
each node finds an estimate of the source of the other node to within a
prescribed distortion. What is the rate–distortion region?

• Again assume that node 1 sends the first index and that the number of rounds
of communication q is even

• A (2nr1, 2nr2, . . . , 2nrq, n) is defined as for the lossless case except here a
rate–distortion tuple (R1, R2, D1, D2) is achievable if there exists a sequence of
(2nr1, 2nr2, . . . , 2nrq, n) codes such that Rj = �

lj∈{j,j+2,...,q−2+j} rlj and

lim supn→∞E(dj(Xn
j , ˆXn
j )) ≤Dj, j ∈[1 : 2]

• The q-round rate-distortion region Rq(D1, D2) is the closure of of the set of
achievable rate pairs (R1, R2) pairs such that (R1, R2, D1, D2) is achievable in
no more than q rounds

• The rate–distortion region R(D1, D2) is the closure of the union of set of
achievable rate pairs (R1, R2) pairs such that (R1, R2, D1, D2) is achievable for
some q. Clearly, Rq(D1, D2) ⊆R(D1, D2)

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 20


• Inner bound: Each nodes performs an independent Wyner–Ziv coding on its
source considering the other node’s source as side information

This scheme, which requires only two rounds of communication yields the inner
bound R1 > I(X1; U1|X2), R2 > I(X2; U2|X1) for some p(u1|x1), p(u2|x2),
ˆx1(u1, x2), and ˆx2(u2, x1) such that E(d1(X1, ˆX1)) ≤D1, E(d2(X2, ˆX2)) ≤D2

• Outer bound: Even if each node knows the other node’s source, we must have
R1 ≥I(X1; ˆX1|X2), R2 ≥I(X2, ˆX2|X1) for some p(ˆx1|x1, x2), p(ˆx2|x1, x2)
such that E(d1(X1, ˆX1)) ≤D1 and E(d2(X2, ˆX2)) ≤D2

• The bounds can be tight

Example: Let (X1, X2) be 2-WGN sources each with average power P and with
correlation coefficient ρ. It can be shown that the rate–distortion region for
distortion pair (D1, D2) is the set of rate pairs (R1, R2) such that
R1 ≥R((1 −ρ2)P/D1), R2 ≥R((1 −ρ2)P/D2), and is achieved by two
independent rounds of the Wyner–Ziv coding

• In general, the inner and outer bounds are not tight and interactive
communication is needed to achieve the rate–distortion region. To reduce the
overall rates, the two nodes should interactively build additional correlation
between their knowledge of the sources instead of greedily using two
independent rounds of Wyner–Ziv coding (cf. the lossless case)

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 21

• The q-round rate–distortion region is given by the following

Theorem 4 [4]: The q-round rate–distortion region for the two-way lossy source
coding problem with distortion pair (D1, D2) is the set of rate pairs (R1, R2)
satisfying

R1 ≥I(X1; U q|X2),

R2 ≥I(X2; U q|X1)

for some U q such that for p(uq|x1, x2) = �q
l=1 p(ul|ul−1, xjl), where jl = 1 if l
is odd and jl = 2 if l is even, and ˆx1(uq, x2), ˆx2(uq, x1) such that
E(d1(X1, ˆX1)) ≤D1, |Uj| ≤|Xlj| · | �j−1
l=1 Ul| + 1, and E(d2(X2, ˆX2)) ≤D2

• Note that the above region is computable for bounded q. However, there is no
bound on q that holds in general and therefore the region is not computable in
general

• Achievability is proved by using the Wyner–Ziv coding in each round

Fix a joint pmf on U q given X1 and X2 and reconstruction functions of the
form specified in the theorem that satisfy the desired distortions

In odd round l ∈[1 : q], node 1 sends the bin index of the description Ul of X1
given U l−1 to node 2 at rate r1l > I(X1; Ul|U l−1, X2), and in even rounds node
2 sends the index of the description Ul of X2 at rate r2l > I(X2; Ul|U l−1, X1)

with equality

Two-Way Source Coding Through a Relay

• Consider the following communication setting motivated by wireless mesh
networks. Two nodes wish to communicate through a relay. Communication is
performed in rounds, where at each round each node sends a codeword to the
relay that depends on its information source and past received sequences and
the relay broadcasts a codeword to the two nodes that depends on its received
sequences. What are the necessary and sufficient conditions for reliable
communication between the two nodes?

Node 1
Node 2
Relay
Xn
1
ˆXn
2

Xn
2
ˆXn
1

M1l(Xn
1 , M l−1
3
)
M2l(Xn
2 , M l−1
3
)

M3l(M l
1, M l
2)
• Let’s first consider a lossless source coding version of this problem. Let (X1, X2)
be 2-DMS. Node 1 observes X1 and node 2 observes X2. What is the minimum
achievable sum-rate Rsum(D1, D2), which is the minimum number of
bits/symbol-pair that need to be communicated so that each node can losslessly
estimate the other node’s source

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 30


• A simple upper bound on R∗
sum can be achieved by having each node perform
Slepian-Wolf coding on its source and sending its index to the relay. The relay
then simply broadcasts these two indices to both nodes. This yields the upper
bound R∗
sum ≤2(H(X1|X2) + H(X2|X1))

• Using a cutset argument, we can show that
R∗
sum ≥H(X1|X2) + H(X2|X1) + max{H(X1|X2), H(X2|X1)} (Why?)

• Now we show that the lower bound is achievable via Slepian-Wolf coding and
“network coding” (see Lecture 16 for more on network coding)

We use Slepian-Wolf coding. Node 1 sends a k1-bit bin index sequence U k1
1
and
node 2 sends a k2-bit bin index sequence U k2
2 . Without loss of generality,
assume that k1 ≥k2. The relay pads U k2
2
by zeros to obtain the k1-bit
sequence ˜U k1
2 . It then adds the two sequences mod 2 and broadcasts the index
V k1 = U k1
1 ⊕˜U k2
2 . Upon receiving V k1, each node recovers the other node’s
index and decodes its message using joint typicality. Following the achievability
proof of the Slepian-Wolf theorem if k1 > H(X1|X2) + δ(ǫ) and
k2 > H(X2|X1) + δ(ǫ), the probability of decoding error →0 as n →∞, and
any sum rate R > 2H(X1|X2) + H(X2|X1) + 3δ(ǫ) is achievable

LNIT: Source Coding over Noiseless Networks (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 21 – 31

• Now, consider the lossy coding formulation of the above problem. Assume that
each node wishes to estimate the other node’s source to some prescribed
distortion. Following two-way lossy source coding and using network coding at
the relay, we can obtain an upper bound on the sum rate for q rounds. Is this
upper bound optimal?

• Channel coding model for the above setting is studied in [5]

Page 21 – 32

Introduction

• In many distributed systems, such as multiprocessors, peer-to-peer networks,
networked agents, and sensor networks, the purpose of communication is not to
communicate or store data, but rather to compute a function, make a decision,
or coordinate an action based on distributed information. How much
communication is needed to perform such a task?

• This problem has been studied in computer science (communication complexity,
gossip), control and optimization (distributed consensus), and information
theory (coding for computing, µ-sum problem)

• We present some of the information theoretic work on this problem and briefly
discuss connections to related work in computer science and control

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 2


Coding for Lossy Computing with Side Information

• Consider a lossy source coding with side information setting for a 2-DMS (X, Y )
(cf. Lecture Notes 12) and distortion measure d(z, ˆz). Suppose that the receiver
wishes to estimate a function Z = g(X, Y ) of the 2-DMS with distortion D.
What is the set of necessary and sufficient rates, i.e., the rate-distortion region
for computing g with distortion D?

• We define a (2nR, n) code, achievability, and the rate distortion function Rg(D)
as in Lecture Notes 12

Page 22 – 3

• Following the proof steps of the Wyner-Ziv theorem, we can determine the
rate–distortion function

Theorem 1 [1]: The rate–distortion function for computing Z = g(X, Y ) is

Rg(D) =
min
p(u|x), ˆz(u,y) I(X; U |Y ),

where the minimum is over p(u|x) with |U| ≤|X| + 1 and ˆz(u, y) such that
E(d(Z, ˆZ)) ≤D

• Example: Let (X, Y ) be 2-WGN(P, ρ) sources and d be the squared error
distortion. If g(X, Y ) = (X + Y )/2, then Rg(D) = R((1 −ρ2)P/4D)

Coding for Lossless Computing with Side Information

• Now suppose the receiver wishes to estimate the function Z = g(X, Y )
losslessly. What is the optimal rate R∗
g?

• Clearly,
H(Z|Y ) ≤R∗
g ≤H(X|Y )

• The bounds sometimes coincide, e.g., when Z = X, or when (X, Y ) are
DSBS(p) and g(X, Y ) = X ⊕Y

• The bounds do not coincide in general, however

Example [2]: Let X = (V1, V2, . . . , V10), where the Vj, j ∈[1 : 10], are i.i.d.
Bern(1/2), and Y ∼Unif[1 : 10]. Suppose g(X, Y ) = VY
The lower bound gives H(VY |Y ) = 1 bit and the upper bound gives
H(X|Y ) = 10 bits

It can be shown that R∗
g = 10 bits

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 5

• Following arguments from the proof of the optimal rate for the causal lossless
source coding with side information (cf. Lecture Notes 12), we can consider the
lossy setting with Hamming distortion measure when D = 0, and show that

R∗
g = Rg(0) = min
p(u|x) I(X; U |Y ),

where the minimum is over p(u|x) such that H(Z|U, Y ) = 0

• Orlitsky and Roche [2] obtained a refined expression for R∗
g. To describe their
result, we need the following definitions:

A set of nodes of a graph G = (N, E) is independent if no two nodes are
connected to each other through an edge. An independent set is maximally
independent if it is not a subset of another independent set. Let Γ(G) be the
collection of maximally independent sets of G

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 6


Example: Consider the graph in the figure.
Γ(G) = {{1, 3, 5}, {1, 3, 6}, {1, 4, 6}, {2, 5}, {2, 6}} (check)

1
2

3

4
5
6

Let X be a random variable over the nodes of the graph G. Define the random
variable W ∈Γ(G) with conditional pmf p(w|x) such that p(w|x) = 0 if x /∈w,
and hence for every x, �

w:x∈w p(w|x) = 1. The graph entropy of X [3] is
defined as
HG(X) := min
p(w|x) I(X; W)

Examples:

◦Graph with no edges, i.e., E = ∅: Here HG(X) = 0 (Γ(G) = {N} has a
single element)

◦Complete graph: Here HG(X) = H(X) (take W = {X})

◦Let X be uniformly distributed over {1, 2, 3} and G has a single edge {1, 3}.
Γ(G) = {{1, 2}, {2, 3}}. By convexity of mutual information, I(X; W) is

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 7

minimized when p({1, 2}|2) = p({2, 3}|2) = 1/2. Thus,

HG(X) = H(W) −H(W |X) = 1 −1

3 = 2

3

Now, let (X, Y ) be two random variables and let the nodes of the graph G be
the support set of X. The graph entropy of X given Y is defined as

HG(X|Y ) := min
p(w|x) I(X; W |Y ),

where the minimum is over p(w|x) such that W ∈Γ(G) and p(w|x) = 0 if
x /∈w

Examples:

◦Graph with no edges, i.e., E = ∅: Here HG(X|Y ) = 0

◦Complete graph: Here HG(X|Y ) = H(X|Y )

◦Let (X, Y ) be uniformly distributed over {(x, y) : x, y ∈{1, 2, 3}, x ̸= y}
and G has a single edge {1, 3}. Γ(G) = {{1, 2}, {2, 3}}. By convexity,
I(X; W|Y ) is minimized when p({1, 2}|2) = p({2, 3}|2) = 1/2. Thus,

HG(X|Y ) = H(W |Y ) −H(W |X, Y ) = 1

3 + 2

3 = 2

3 · H(1/4)

Finally, define the characteristic graph G of the (X, Y, g) tuple [4] with nodes

Page 22 – 8


over the support set of X such that distinct nodes x, x′ are connected if there is
an y such that p(x, y), p(x′, y) > 0 and g(x, y) ̸= g(x′, y)

• Orlitsky–Roche Theorem [2]: The optimal rate for losslessly computing of
function g is
R∗
g = HG(X|Y )

Distributed Coding for Lossless Computing

• Consider a distributed lossless source coding setting for a 2-DMS (X1, X2) (cf.
Lecture Notes 11). Suppose that the receiver wishes to losslessly compute a

• This problem is in general open

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 10


• Simple inner and outer bounds

◦Inner bound: Clearly, the Slepian–Wolf region

R1 ≥H(X1|X2),

R2 ≥H(X2|X1),

R1 + R2 ≥H(X1, X2)

constitutes an inner bound to the rate region for any function g(X1, X2)

◦Outer bound: Even when the receiver knows X1, R2 ≥H(Z|X1) is still
necessary. Similarly, we must have R1 ≥H(Z|X2). These constraints
constitute the following outer bound on the rate region for any function
Z = g(X1, X2),

R1 ≥H(Z|X2),

R2 ≥H(Z|X1)

• The inner bound is sometimes tight, e.g., when Z = (X1, X2). In [5], necessary
and sufficient conditions are given for the inner bound to be tight

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 11

• The outer bound is sometimes tight. Consider the following:

Example [6]: Let (X1, X2) be DSBS(p), thus X1 ∼Bern(1/2) and
Z ∼Bern(p) are independent and X2 = X1 ⊕Z. Let
g(X1, X2) = X1 ⊕X2 = Z

The inner bound reduces to R1 ≥H(p), R2 ≥H(p), R1 + R2 ≥1 + H(p),
while the outer bound reduces to R1 ≥H(p) and R2 ≥H(p)

We now show that the outer bound is achievable using random linear codes!

◦Codebook generation: Randomly generate k × n binary matrix A with i.i.d.
Bern(1/2) entries

◦Encoding: Sender 1 sends the binary k-vector A Xn
1 and Sender 2 sends the
binary k-vector A Xn
2
◦Decoding: The receiver adds the two binary k-vectors to obtain
A Xn
1 ⊕A Xn
2 = A Zn

◦It can be shown that if k/n > H(p) + δ(ǫ), then the probability of decoding
error averaged over the choice of the encoding matrix →0 as n →∞(why?)

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 12


• Remark: The above simple outer bound can be improved using the
Orlitsky–Roche Theorem twice, once when the receiver knows X1 and a second
time when it knows X2. Defining the characteristic graphs G1 for (X1, X2, g)
and G2 for (X2, X1, g) as before, we obtain the outer bound R1 ≥HG1(X1|X2),
R2 ≥HG2(X2|X1)

• Remarks

◦When µ1µ2 = 0, then the µ-sum problem is equivalent to the quadratic
Gaussian distributed source coding (Lecture Notes 13) with D1 ≥1 or
D2 ≥1. The rate distortion region is given by Oohama [8] with proper
normalization. Thus, for µ1µ2 ≥0, the Berger–Tung inner bound is tight

◦On the other hand, when µ1µ2 < 0, it can be shown that the Berger–Tung
inner bound is not tight in general [9]

Two-Way Coding for Lossy Computing

• Consider a two-way lossy source coding setting for 2-DMS (cf. Lecture Notes

• Simple inner and outer bounds:

◦Inner bound: Using two independent rounds of Wyner-Ziv coding, we obtain
the inner bound R1 > I(X1; U1|X2), R2 > I(X2; U1|X1) for some p(u1|x1),
p(u2|x2), ˆz1(u1, x2), and ˆz2(u2, x1) such that
E(d1(Z1, ˆZ1)) ≤D1, E(d2(Z2, ˆZ2)) ≤D2

◦Outer bound: Even if each encoder knows the other node’s source, we must
have R1 ≥I(Z2; ˆZ2|X2), R2 ≥I(Z1; ˆZ1|X1) for some p(ˆz1|x1, x2),
p(ˆz2|x1, x2) such that E(d1(Z1, ˆZ1)) ≤D1 and E(d2(Z2, ˆZ2)) ≤D2

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 19

• The bounds can be tight

Example [10]: Let (X, Y ) be a 2-WGN(P, ρ) source let
Z1 = Z2 = Z = (X1 + X2)/2. The rate–distortion region for distortion D is the
set of of rate pairs (R1, R2) such that R1 ≥R((1 −ρ2)P/4D),
R2 ≥R((1 −ρ2)P/4D). It is easy to see that this region coincides with the
inner bound. It can also be shown that it coincides with the outer bound (why?)

• The bounds are not tight in general. Following the two-way lossy source coding
theorem (cf. Lecture Notes 21), we can readily establish the following
rate–distortion region for q rounds of communication

Theorem 4: The q-round rate–distortion region for computing functions Z1 and
Z2 with distortion pair (D1, D2) is the set of rate pairs (R1, R2) satisfying

R1 ≥I(X1; U q|X2),

R2 ≥I(X2; U q|X1)

for some p(uq|x1, x2) = �

l=1 p(ul|ul−1, xjl), jl = 1 if l is odd and jl = 2 if l is
even, ˆz1(uq, x1) and ˆz2(uq, x2) such that E(d1(Z1, ˆZ1)) ≤D1 and
E(d2(Z2, ˆZ2)) ≤D2

• Note that the above region is computable for bounded q. However, there is no
bound on the cardinality of q in general

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 20


Two-Way Coding for Lossless Computing

• Consider the two-way lossless source coding setting in Lecture Notes 21, where
node 1 wishes to losslessly estimate Z1 = g1(X1, X2) and node 2 wishes to
losslessly estimate Z2 = g2(X1, X2). What is the optimal rate region for this
setting?

• The theorem for the lossy setting can be specialized to yield the following
characterization of the optimal rate region for q rounds

Theorem 5 [2, 11]: The optimal q-round rate region for lossless computing of
the functions Z1 and Z2 is the set of rate pairs (R1, R2) satisfying

R1 ≥I(X1; U q|X2),

R2 ≥I(X2; U q|X1)

for some p(uq|x1, x2) = �
l=1 p(ul|ul−1, xjl), jl = 1 if l is odd and jl = 2 if l is
even, such that H(g1(X1, X2)|X1, U q) = 0 and H(g2(X1, X2)|X2, U q) = 0

• In some cases, 2 independent rounds is sufficient, e.g., if Z1 = X2 and
Z2 = X1, or when (X1, X2) is a DSBS and Z1 = Z2 = X1 ⊕X2 (Why?)

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 21

• In general, interactivity can reduce transmission

Assume Z1 = Z and Z2 = ∅and consider 2 rounds of communication; in round
1, node 1 sends a message to node 2 that depends on X1 and in round 2, node
2 sends a second message to node 1 that depends on X2 and the first message

By Theorem 5, the optimal rate region for 2 rounds of communication is the set
of rate pairs (R1, R2) such that
R1 ≥I(U1; X1|X2),

R2 ≥I(U2; X2|U1, X1)
for some p(u1|x1)p(u2|u1, x2) satisfying H(Z|U1, U2, X1) = 0

The following example shows that 2 rounds can be better than one round

Example [2]: Let X1 be a Bern(p) source for p ≪1/2, X2 be Bern(1/2), and
Z = X1 · X2. The minimum rate for one round of communication from node 2
to node 1 is R2 = 1 bit/ symbol

Consider the following 2-round coding scheme. In the first round, node 1 uses
Slepian–Wolf coding to send X1 losslessly to node 2. This succeeds provided
R1 > H(p). In the second round, node 2 sets Y = X2 if X1 = 1 and Y = e if
X1 = 0. It then uses Slepian-Wolf coding to send Y losslessly to node 1. This
succeeds provided R2 > H(Y |X1) = p. Thus the sum rate for the two rounds is
R1 + R2 = H(p) + p < 1 for sufficiently small p

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 22


• More than 2 rounds may reduce transmission further

Example [11]: Let (X1, X2) be DSBS(p) and
g1(X1, X2) = g2(X1, X2) = X1 · X2. For two rounds of communication,
R1 > HG(X1|X2) = H(X1|X2) = H(p) and
R2 > H(g1(X1, X2)|X1) = (1/2)H(p) are achievable. Optimality follows by the
Orlitsky–Roche Theorem for the R1 bound and by the cutset bound for the R2
bound

Consider the following 3-round coding scheme. Set the auxiliary random
variables in the above theorem as U1 = (1 −X1) · W , where W ∼Bern(1/2) is
independent of X1, U2 = X2 · (1 −U1), and U3 = X1 · U2. Since U3 = X1 · X2,
both nodes can compute the product noiselessly. This gives the upper bound on
the sum rate

R1 + R2 < 5

In [11] it is shown that more rounds improve the sum rate further and an upper
bound on the sum rate is provided for an infinite number of rounds

• The two-way coding for computing problem is closely related to communication
complexity introduced in [12]. In this setup both nodes wish to compute the
same function with no errors. Work in this area is described in [13]

Cascade Coding for Computing

• Consider the following lossy source coding for computing problem for 2-DMS
(X1, X2) [14]. The decoder wishes to reconstruct the function Z = g(X1, X2)
with respect to distortion measure d(z, ˆz). What is the rate–distortion region?

Encoder 1

Xn

M1

• The rate–distortion region is not known in general, even when X1 and X2 are
independent! We discuss inner and outer bounds

• Cutset outer bound: If a rate pair (R1, R2) is achievable at distortion D, then it
must satisfy the conditions

R1 ≥I(X1; U |X2),

R2 ≥I(Z; ˆZ)

for some p(u|x1)p(ˆz|x2, u) such that E(d(Z, ˆZ)) ≤D

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 24


• Local computing inner bound: Encoder 1 uses Wyner-Ziv coding to send a
description U of its source X1 to encoder 2 at rate R1 > I(X1; U|X2). Encoder
2 sends the estimate ˆZ based on (U, X2) at rate R2 > I(X2, U; ˆZ) to the
decoder. This gives the local computing inner bound for distortion D that
consists of all rate pairs (R1, R2) satisfying

R1 > I(X1; U |X2),

R2 > I(X2, U; ˆZ)

for some p(u|x1)p(ˆz|x2, u) such that E(d(Z, ˆZ)) ≤D

• The local computing bound coincides with the cutset bound for the special case
of lossless computing of Z. To show this, let d be the Hamming distortion
measure and consider the case of D = 0. This yields

R1 > I(X1; U |X2),

R2 > H(Z)

for some p(u|x1) such that H(Z|U, X2) = 0. Now, consider the cutset bound
and set ˆZ = Z. Since ˆZ →(X2, U) →X1 form a Markov chain and Z is a
function of (X1, X2), we must have the condition H(Z|U, X2) = 0

As in the Orlitsky–Roche Theorem, the condition R1 > I(X1; U|X2) can be
further simplified to a graph entropy

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 25

• Forwarding inner bound: Consider the following alternative coding scheme.
Encoder 1 again sends a description U1 of its source X1 at rate
R1 > I(X1; U1|X2). Encoder 2 forwards the description from encoder 1
together with a description U2 of its source X2 given U1 at total rate
R2 > I(X1; U1) + I(X2; U2|U1). The decoder then computes the estimate
ˆZ(U1, U2). This yields the forwarding inner bound for distortion D consisting of
all rate pairs (R1, R2) satisfying

R1 > I(X1; U1|X2),

R2 > I(U1; X1) + I(U2; X2|U1)

for some p(u1|x1)p(u2|x2, u1) and ˆz(u1, u2) such that E(d(Z, ˆZ)) ≤D

• Forwarding is optimal when X1 and X2 are independent and Z = (X1, X2)

• The lossless computing example shows that local computing can outperform
forwarding (check). We give another example where this is the case

Example [14]: Let X1, X2 be independent WGN(1) sources, d be squared error
distortion, and Z = X1 + X2

• Combined inner bound: Local computing and forwarding can be combined as
follows. Encoder 1 uses Wyner-Ziv coding to send the description pair (U, V ) of

• Cascade source coding versus distributed source coding for computing:

◦The lossless computing rate region is known for the cascade case but is not
known in general for the distributed source coding setting. This apparently
suggests that the cascade problem is in general more tractable than
distributed source coding for computing

◦This is not the case, however. Consider the µ-sum problem. While the
rate–distortion region for WGN sources is known for the distributed source
coding setting (see Lecture Notes 13), it is not known for the cascade case,
even when X1 and X2 are independent

Page 22 – 29

Distributed Lossy Averaging

• The distributed averaging problem is a canonical example of distributed
consensus studied in control and computer science [15, 16, 17]. We consider the
following lossy source coding formulation for this problem

• Consider a noiseless network modeled by a graph with N nodes and a set of
undirected edges representing two-way communication links

• Node j ∈[1 : N] observes an independent WGN(1) source Xj

• Each node wishes to estimate the average Z := (1/N) �N

• Communication is performed in q rounds. In each round an edge (node-pair) is

detailed previously)

• Let rl be the total communication rate in round l ∈[1 : q] and define the

Computing over a MAC

• As we have discussed in Lecture Notes 15, source–channel separation holds
when sending independent sources over a DM-MAC, but does not necessarily
hold when the sources are correlated. We show through an example that even
when the sources are independent, separation does not necessarily hold when the
goal is to compute a function of the sources over a DM-MAC

• Example [18]: Let (U1, U2) be a pair of independent Bern(1/2) sources and
suppose we wish to compute Z = U1 ⊕U2 over a modulo-2 sum DM-MAC
followed by a BSC(p)
What are the sufficient and necessary conditions to compute Zk losslessly by
using the MAC n times (i.e., the transmission rate is r = k/n)?

U k

• With separate source–channel coding, Z = U1 ⊕U2 can be computed losslessly
over the DM-MAC at rate r if and only if

rH(Z|U2) = rH(U1) ≤I(X1; Y |X2, Q),

rH(U2) ≤I(X2; Y |X1, Q),

r(H(U1) + H(U2)) ≤I(X1, X2; Y |Q)

for some p(q)p(x1|q)p(x2|q)

This reduces to r(H(U1) + H(U2)) = 2r ≤1 −H(p). Thus, r is achievable via
separate source–channel coding if and only if r ≤(1 −H(p))/2

On the other hand, considering the cutset at the input of the BSC part of the
channel gives the upper bound

rH(U1 ⊕U2) ≤1 −H(p)

We now show that the upper bound is achievable using random linear codes:

◦Codebook generation: Randomly and independently generate a l × k binary
matrix A and an n × l binary matrix B each with i.i.d. Bern(1/2) entries

◦Encoding: Encoder 1 sends Xn
1 = BAU k

probability of error (averaged over the random matrix B) →0 as n →∞(cf.
channel coding with linear codes in Lecture Notes 3)

If l > k(1 + δ(ǫ)), the receiver can decode Zk with probability of error
(averaged over the random matrix A) →0 as n →∞. Therefore,
r < 1 −H(p) is achievable using joint source–channel coding

Key New Ideas and Techniques

• Information theoretic framework for distributed computing

• Graph entropy and conditional graph entropy

• Interaction reduces communication requirement for computing

• Source–channel separation is suboptimal even when computing from
independent sources

• Open problem: What is the optimal rate region for distributed computing?

Appendix: Proof of Orlitsky–Roche Theorem

• We prove that
min
p(u|x) I(U; X|Y ) = min
p(w|x) I(W; X|Y ),

where the first minimum is over all p(u|x) with H(Z|U, Y ) = 0 and the second
minimum is over all p(w|x) such that W ∈Γ(G) and X ∈W

• Consider p(w|x) that achieves the second minimum. Since w is a maximally
independent set with respect to the characteristic graph of (X, Y, g), for each
y ∈Y, g(x, y) is constant for all x ∈w with p(x, y) > 0. Hence, g(x, y) can be
uniquely determined from (w, y) whenever p(w, x, y) > 0, or equivalently,
H(g(X, Y )|W, Y ) = 0. By identifying U = W , it follows that
minp(u|x) I(U; X|Y ) ≤minp(w|x) I(W; X|Y )

• To show the other direction of inequality, let p(u|x) achieve the first minimum
and define
w(u) := {x : p(u, x) > 0}

◦If p(w, x) > 0, then there exists a u such that w(u) = w and p(u, x) > 0. By
definition, x ∈w

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 40


◦Also W →X →Y form a Markov chain, since U →X →Y form a Markov
chain and W is a function of U

◦Furthermore, suppose p(w) > 0, i.e., there exists a u such that p(u) > 0 and
w(u) = w. Now if x ∈w, then p(u, x) > 0. Hence by Markovity p(x, y) > 0
implies p(u, x, y) > 0. But since H(g(X, Y )|U, Y ) = 0, (u, y) should
determine g(x, y) uniquely. It follows that if x, x′ ∈w and
p(x, y), p(x′, y) > 0, then g(x, y) = g(x′, y), which means that W is
maximally independent

◦Finally, since I(W; X|Y ) ≤I(W, U; X|Y ) ≤I(U; X|Y ), we have
minp(u|x) I(U; X|Y ) ≥minp(w|x) I(W; X|Y ), which completes the proof

LNIT: Communication for Computing (2010-03-20 20:37)
Page 22 – 41
