Shannon’s Secrecy System

• A sender (Alice) wishes to communicate a message M ∈[1 : 2nR] to a receiver
(Bob) over a public channel in the presence of an eavesdropper (Eve) who
observes the channel output. Alice and Bob share a key K ∈Z+, which is
unknown to Eve. How many key bits (H(K)) are needed so that Eve cannot
obtain any information about the message ?

M
L
ˆ
M
Encoder
Decoder

Eavesdropper

K

• The message M ∼Unif[1 : 2nR]

• The encoder (encryption function) assigns a ciphertext l(m, k) to each message
m ∈[1 : 2nR] and key k, and the decoder (decryption function) assigns a
message ˆm(l, k) to each ciphertext l and key k

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 2


• The communication system is said to have perfect secrecy if

◦P{M ̸= ˆ
M(L(M, K), K)} = 0, i.e., the message is decoded correctly, and

◦The information leakage I(L; M) = 0, i.e., the ciphertext reveals no
information about the message

• Theorem 1 (Shannon’s Perfect Secrecy Theorem) [1]: The sufficient and
necessary condition for perfect secrecy is H(K) ≥H(M)

• Proof: To prove necessity, consider

H(M)
(a)
= H(M |L)

≤H(M, K|L)

(b)
= H(K|L)

≤H(K),

where (a) follows by the secrecy constraint I(M; L) = 0 and (b) follows from
the communication constraint P{M ̸= ˆ
M} = 0

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 3

To prove sufficiency, let the key K be uniformly distributed over [1 : 2nR] and
take L = M + K mod 2⌊nR⌋, or equivalently, take the bitwise XOR of the
binary expansions of M and K (so called one-time pad). It can be readily
checked that M and L are independent. To recover the message M from the
ciphertext L, the receiver simply subtract the key from the ciphertext to recover
M = L −K mod 2⌊nR⌋

• So this is bad news! Alice and Bob need to share a key as long as the message
itself

How do they share the long key to begin with?

• Several directions to avoid this limitation of Shannon’s secrecy system

◦Computational security: how to exploit the computational hardness of
recovering the message from the ciphertext without knowing the key
(assuming P ̸= NP ) [2, 3]?

◦Wiretap channel: how to use the better quality of Alice–Bob channel to outdo
eve

◦Secret key generation: how to agree upon a secret key (common randomness)
from correlated random variables?

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 4


Secure Communication over a DMC

• Consider the following extension of the Shannon secrecy system to DMC

M
Xn
Y n
ˆ
M
Encoder
Decoder

Eavesdropper

K

p(y|x)

• Formally, A (2nR, 2nRK, n) secrecy code for the Shannon system consists of:

1. A message set [1 : 2nR] and a key set [1 : 2nRK]

2. A randomized encoder that generates codeword Xn(m, k) ∈X n according to
the conditional pmf p(xn|m, k) for each message–key pair
(m, k) ∈[1 : 2nR] × [1 : 2nRK]

3. A decoder that assigns a message ˆm(yn, k) or an error message e to each
received sequence yn ∈Yn and key k ∈[1 : 2nRK]

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 5

• We assume that the message–key pair (M, K) is uniformly distributed over
[1 : 2nR] × [1 : 2nRK]

• The information leakage rate associated with the (2nR, 2nRK, n) secrecy code is
defined as
R(n)
l
= 1

nI(M; Y n)

• The average probability of error for the secrecy code is defined as

P (n)
e
= P{M ̸= ˆ
M(Y n, K)}

• A rate R is achievable at key rate RK if there exists a sequence of
(2nR, 2nRK, n) codes with R(n)
l
→0 and P (n)
e
→0 as n →∞

• The secrecy capacity C(RK) of the DMC is the supremum of all achievable
rates at key rate RK

• This asymptotic notion of secrecy is weaker than Shannon’s original notion of
perfect secrecy. Can we send at a higher rate?

• Theorem 2 [1]: The secrecy capacity is

C(RK) = min{RK, max
p(x) I(X; Y )}

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 6


Thus secure communication is still limited by the key rate until saturated by the
channel capacity

• What will happen if the enemy does not receive the same channel output but
listens through another channel? Can we achieve a positive rate without a
shared key?

The Wiretap Channel

• A discrete-memoryless wiretap channel (DM-WTC) (X, p(y, z|x), Y × Z) is a
DM-BC with sender X, legitimate receiver Y and eavesdropper Z

• The sender X wishes to send a message M ∈[1 : 2nR] reliably to the receiver
Y while keeping it secret from the eavesdropper Z

Encoder
M

ˆ
M
Decoder

Eavesdropper

Y n

Zn

p(y, z|x)
Xn

• A (2nR, n) secrecy code for the DM-WTC consists of:

1. A message set [1 : 2nR]

2. A randomized encoder that generates a codeword Xn(m), m ∈[1 : 2nR],
according to p(xn|m)

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 8


3. A decoder that assigns a message ˆm(yn) or an error message e to each
received sequence yn ∈Yn

• The message M ∼Unif[1 : 2nR]

• The information leakage rate is defined as R(n)
l
:= (1/n)I(M; Zn)

• The probability of error, achievability, and the secrecy capacity CS are defined as
for the problem of secure communication over DMC

• Theorem 3 [4]: The secrecy capacity of the DM-WTC (X, p(y, z|x), Y × Z) is
given by

CS = max
p(u,x) (I(U; Y ) −I(U; Z))

for |U| ≤|X|

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 9

• Special cases:

◦The wiretap channel was first introduced by Wyner [5], who assumed that the
channel to the eavesdropper is a physically degraded version of the channel to
the receiver, i.e. p(y, z|x) = p(y|x)p(z|y), Under this assumption,

I(U; Y ) −I(U; Z) = I(U; Y |Z)

≤I(X; Y |Z)

= I(X; Y ) −I(X; Z)

Hence, the secrecy capacity in this case is

CS = max
p(x) (I(X; Y ) −I(X; Z))

It can be shown that the secrecy capacity depends only on the marginal pmfs
p(y|x) and p(z|x). Hence, the above result generalizes to stochastically
degraded DM-WTC

◦The above result holds if Y is more capable than Z. To show this consider

I(U; Y ) −I(U; Z) = I(X; Y ) −I(X; Z) −(I(X; Y |U) −I(X; Z|U))

≤I(X; Y ) −I(X; Z),

since the more capable condition implies that I(X; Y |U) −I(X; Z|U) ≥0
for all p(u, x)

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 10


Examples

• Binary symmetric wiretap channel [6]: Consider the BS-WTC: Yi = Xi ⊕Z1i,
and Zi = Xi ⊕Z2i, where {Z1i} and {Z2i} are independent Bern(p1) and
Bern(p2) processes, respectively

The secrecy capacity for this channel is

CS =

�
H(p2) −H(p1)
for p2 ≥p1,
0
otherwise

Proof: When p1 > p2, The eavesdropper can decode any message that the
receiver can decode, thus CS = 0

When p2 ≥p1, capacity can be achieved using binary-symmetric random codes

To prove the converse, let X ∼Bern(p) and consider

I(X; Y ) −I(X; Z) = H(p2) −H(p1) −(H(p ∗p2) −H(p ∗p1))

For p < 1/2, p ∗ǫ is monotonically increasing in ǫ. This implies that
H(p ∗p2) −H(p ∗p1) ≥0. Thus setting p = 1/2 optimizes the above expression

Extensions

• A secret key between the sender and receiver can be used to further increase the
secrecy capacity. If RK is the rate of the secret key, then the secrecy capacity of
a wiretap channel p(y, z|x) is

CS(RK) = max
p(u,x) min{I(U; Y ) −I(U; Z) + RK, I(U; Y )}

Sometimes interaction between the sender and receiver can generate a shared
key. Ahlswede and Cai [9] and Ardestanizadeh et al. [10] studied a secure
feedback coding scheme to generate a shared key, which is optimal when the
wiretap channel is physically degraded. We will soon see that even nonsecure
interaction can provide a secret key

• The secrecy capacity for more than 2 receivers is not known. In [11], a lower
bound on the secrecy capacity for a 2-receivers, 1-eavesdropper wiretap channel
is established. We briefly discuss this result as it involves several ideas beyond
wiretap channel coding

Consider a general 3-receiver DM-BC with sender X, two legitimate receivers
Y1, Y2 and an eavesdropper Z. The sender wishes to send a message
M ∈[1 : 2nR] reliably to Y1 and Y2 while keeping it asymptotically secret from
Z, i.e., limn→∞I(M; Zn)/n = 0

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 21

A straightforward extension of the wiretap result to 3-receivers yields the lower
bound
CS ≥max
p(u,x) min{I(U; Y1) −I(U; Z), I(U; Y2) −I(U; Z)}

Now, suppose Z is a degraded version of Y1, then from the wiretap result,
I(U; Y1) −I(U; Y3) ≤I(X; Y1) −I(X; Z) for all p(u, x). However, no such
inequality holds in general for the second term. Using indirect decoding [12], we
can replace U with X in the first term while keeping U in the second term.
This can increase the rate (see example in Lecture notes 5). To prove
achievability of this rate, we randomly and independently generate sequences
un(l0), l0 ∈[1 : 2n ˜
R], each generated according to �n
i=1 p(ui). Partition the set
[1 : 2n ˜
R] into 2nR bins B(m) m ∈[1 : 2nR]. For each un(l0), conditionally
independently generate 2nR1 sequences xn(l0, l1), l1 ∈[1 : 2nR1], each according
to �n
i=1 p(xi|ui). To send a message m ∈[1 : 2nR], choose an index pair
(L0, L1) uniformly at random from B(m) × [1 : 2nR1], and transmit xn(L0, L1).
Receiver Y2 decodes m directly through U and receiver Y1 decodes m indirectly
through X. It can be shown that the probability of error →0 as n →∞if

˜R < I(U; Y2) −δ(ǫ), ˜R + R1 < I(X; Y1) −δ(ǫ)

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 22


Further, it can be shown that M is kept asymptotically secret from the
eavesdropper Z if
˜R −R > I(U; Z) + δ(ǫ), R1 > I(X; Z|U) + δ(ǫ)

Eliminating ˜R and R1 using the Fourier-Motzkin procedure, we obtain the lower
bound
CS ≥max
p(u,x) min{I(X; Y1) −I(X; Z), I(U; Y2) −I(U; Z)}

This bound can be shown to be tight for the BEC example in Lecture notes 5
and is strictly larger than the straightforward extension of the
Csisz´ar–K¨orner–Marton secrecy capacity result

The above bound can be improved via Marton coding and time-sharing to
obtain the following lower bound:

Theorem 4:

CS ≥min{I(U1; Y1|Q) −I(U1; Z|Q), I(U2; Y2|Q) −I(U2; Z|Q), 1

2(I(U1; Y1|Q)

+ I(U2; Y2|Q) −2I(U0; Z|Q) −I(U1; U2|U0))}
for some p(q, u0)p(u1|u0)p(x, u2|u0, u1) = p(q, u0)p(u2|u0)p(x, u1|u0, u2) such
that I(U1, U2; Z|U0) ≤I(U1; Z|U0) + I(U2; Z|U0) −I(U1; U2|U0). This bound
was shown to be optimal for reversely degraded BC with an arbitrarily number
of sub-channels and when both Y1 and Y2 are less noisy than Z [11]

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 23

• Broadcast channel with common and confidential messages: In [4], it is assumed
that there is a common message M0 that needs to be sent reliably to both the
receiver Y and eavesdropper Z in addition to a confidential message M1 that
needs to be sent to Y and kept partially secret from Z

Formally, a (2nR0, 2nR1, 2nRl, n) code for the DM-BC with common and
confidential messages consists of: (i) two message sets [1 : 2nR0] and [1 : 2nR1];
(ii) an encoder that randmly assigns a codeword Xn(m0, m1) to each (m0, m1)
according to p(xn|m0, m1); and (iii) two decoders; decoder Y assigns to each
sequence yn an estimate ( ˆ
M01,
ˆ
M11) ∈[1 : 2nR0] × [1 : 2nR1] or an error
message, and decoder Z assigns to each sequence zn an estimate
ˆ
M02 ∈[1 : 2nR0] or an error message. The message pair (M0, M1) are assumed
to be uniformly distributed over the message sets. The probability of error is

P (n)
e
= P{ ˆ
M0j ̸= M0 for j = 1, 2 or ˆ
M1 ̸= M1}

The information leakage rate at receiver Z is I(M1; Zn)/n

A secrecy rate tuple (R0, R1, Rl) is said to be achievable if there exists a
sequence of codes such that P (n)
e
→0 as n →∞and
lim supn→∞
1
nI(M1; Zn) ≤Rl. The secrecy capacity region is the closure of the
set of achievable rate tuples (R0, R1, Rl)

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 24


Theorem 5 [4]: The secrecy capacity region of the 2-receiver DM-BC with
common and confidential messages is the set of rate tuples (R0, R1, Rl) such
that
R0 ≤I(U; Z),

R1 ≤I(V ; Y |U),

R0 + R1 ≤I(V ; Y ),

Rl ≥max{0, R1 −[I(V ; Y |U) −I(V ; Z|U)]+}
for some p(u, v, x) = p(u)p(v|u)p(x|v), where [a]+ := max{a, 0}

In [11], inner bounds on the secrecy capacity regions for 2-receiver and
1-eavesdropper and 1-receiver and 2-eavesdroppers are given. The secrecy
capacity regions for these scenarios are known only for some special cases

• The work on wiretap channel has been extended to other multiple user channels
(see survey paper [13]):

◦BC channels: In [14] inner and outer bounds for the DM-BC with
independent confidential messages is established. Broadcast fading channels
and product channels are considered in [15]

◦Relay channels: In [16] the DM-RC with eavesdropper at the relay is studied,
and in [17], results for DM-RC with eavesdropper at the receiver is studied

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 25

◦MAC: In [18], it is assumed that both transmitters are broadcasting to a
common receiver. Each transmitter can eavesdrop on the other transmitter’s
signal. Transmission of independent confidential messages to the common
receiver is considered. In [19], the Gaussian MAC in which an eavesdropper is
treated as another MAC receiver is considered

◦Interference Channels: Inner and outer bounds for the interference channel
with confidential messages in which each message is to be kept secret from
the unintended receiver is considered in [14]. A model of the interference
channel with secrecy constraint in which a common message is available at
both encoders and a confidential message is available at encoder 2 only,
which is to be kept secret from receiver 1 is studied in [20]

◦Compound channels and channels with state/side information: Results for the
compound wiretap channel are reported in [21]. Achievable rate-information
leakage regions for channels with state or side information available at
encoder or decoder are studied in [22] and [23]

◦Source coding: In [24, 25] the source coding problem when an eavesdropper
observes the output of the encoder, but does not observe the side information
(or the key) is considered. The secrecy measure in this case is the
equivocation the eavesdropper has about the source without knowing the key.
A tradeoff exists between the key rate and equivocation at the eavesdropper

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 26


Secret Key Agreement: The Source Model

• Motivation: If the channel to the eavesdropper is less noisy than the channel to
the receiver, then CS = 0. Thus no secret communication can take place at a
positive rate without a secret key shared between the sender and receiver. It
turned out, however, that it is possible to agree on a secret key over a public
channel if the sender and receiver have access to correlated random variables
work on secret key agreement

• We discuss the secret key agreement under the source model in some detail. We
then briefly discuss the problem under the channel model

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 27

Problem Setup

• Consider a system with two nodes, a single eavesdropper, and 3-DMS
(X1 × X2 × Z, p(x1, x2, z)). Node 1 observes a DMS X1, node 2 observes a
DMS X2, and the eavesdropper observes a DMS Z. The nodes communicate
over a public, noiseless broadcast channel with the goal of agreeing on a secret
key that the eavesdropper is ignorant of. What is the maximum achievable
secret key rate?

• We assume without loss of generality that the nodes communicate in a round
robin fashion over q rounds, where q is even and may depend on n. Thus Node
1 transmits during rounds l = 1, 3, . . . , q −1, and Node 2 transmits during the
rest of the rounds

• A (2nR1, . . . , 2nRq, n) code for the secret key agreement problem consists of:

1. Two randomized encoders, one for each node. In odd rounds
l = 1, 3, . . . , q −1, encoder 1 generates an index Ml ∈[1 : 2nRl] according to
p(ml|xn
1, ml−1), i.e., a random assignment given its source vector and all
previously transmitted indices. Similarly, in even rounds l = 2, 4, . . . , q,
encoder 2, generates an index Ml ∈[1 : 2Rl] according to p(ml|xn
2, ml−1)

2. Two decoders, one for each node. Decoder j = 1, 2 generates a key Kj ∈Z+

according to p(kj|mq, xn
l ), i.e., a random assignment given its source
sequence and all received sequence of indices

• The probability of error for the code is defined as

P (n)

• The key leakage rate is defined as R(n)

(2nR1, 2nR2, . . . , 2nRq, n) codes such that P (n)

• The secret key capacity CK(X1; X2||Z) (or in short when there is no ambiguity
CK) is the supremum of all achievable rates R

generation as C(d)

Secret Key Capacity from One-Way Communication

• We consider the case of one-way communication, where the public
communications is either from node 1 to node 2 or visa versa, that is,
M q = (M1, 0) or (0, M2), and denote the one-way secret key capacity by CK-1

• Clearly CK-1 ≤CK

• Theorem 6 [26]: The one-way secret key capacity for sources (X1, X2, Z) is

CK-1(X1; X2||Z) = max{

stands for “silent”

As will be seen shortly, an upper bound on the key rate is given by
I(X1; X2|Z) = I(X11; X21|Z1) + I(X12; X22|Z2). Note that this is achievable
using the interactive communication achievability bound by setting U1 = X11
and U2 = X21. This, however, is larger than the one-way secrecy key rate CK-1
To show this, first note that the one-way rate from X1 to X2 depends only on
the marginals p(x1, x2) and p(x1, z). From the figure, (X11, X12, X21, X22) has
the same joint pmf as (X11, X12, X21, T2)

Page 23 – 43

Further, (X11, X12) →(X21, T2) →(Z1, Z2) form a Markov chain. This gives

2 ||Z) = CK-1(X1; (X21, T2)(s)||Z)

(a)
= I(X1; X21, T2|Z)

= I(X11; X21|Z1) + I(X12; T2|Z2),
where (a) follows from the bound
CK-1(X1; (X21, T2)(s)||Z) ≥I(X1; X21, T2) −I(X1; Z) = I(X1; X21, T2|Z).
But since the key capacity for (X11, X12) →(X21, T2) →(Z1, Z2) is upper
bounded by I(X1; X21, T2|Z), we have
CK-1(X1; (X21; T2)(s)||Z) = I(X1; X21, T2|Z). This key rate is less than the
upper bound for the original system since
I(X12; T2|Z2) = I(X12; T2) −I(X12; Z2)

= I(X12; T2) −I(X22; Z2)/0.9

< I(X12; T2) −I(X22; Z2)

= I(X12; X22) −I(X22; Z2) = I(X12; X22|Z2)

Upper Bound on 2-Node Secret Key Capacity

• Theorem 8 [29]: The following is an upper bound on the 2-node secret key
capacity for the source (X1, X2, Z)

CK ≤min
p(¯z|z) I(X1; X2| ¯Z),

where (X1, X2) →Z →¯Z form a Markov chain

• The above bound is tight If X1 →X2 →Z or X2 →X1 →Z. This follows
immediately from the achievability bound in one way key rate and Markovity

• Proof:

◦We first consider the case where the encoding and key generation functions

Special Cases

• Consider the secret key agreement problem when nodes 1 and 2 know the
eavesdropper’s sequence Zn, and we wish to find the secret key capacity defined
as before. This models a scenario where we know which nodes are
eavesdropping and have the ability to force these nodes to broadcast their
information to all nodes

For this case the secrecy capacity is

CK( ˜X1; ˜X2||Z) = I(X1; X2|Z)

Achievability follows by defining ˜X1 := (X1, Z), ˜X2 := (X2, Z), and ˜Z = Z,
and using the one-way achievability scheme (setting U = ∅and V = ˜X1). The
converse follows from the above upper bound

• The case where Z = ∅(or Z is independent of (X1, X2)) corresponds to the
case where the eavesdropper listens to the public communication but has no
prior correlated information. This secrecy capacity for this case is I(X1; X2)

Extension to Multiple Nodes

• Consider a network with N nodes, an eavesdropper , and an (N + 1)-DMS
(X1 × X2 × · · · × XN × Z, p(x1, . . . , xN, Z)). Node j ∈[1 : N] observes source
Xj and the eavesdropper observes Z. The nodes communicate in a round robin
fashion over q rounds, where q is divisible by N, such that node j
communicates in times j, N + j, 2N + j, . . . , q −N + j

Kj ∈Z+ according to p(kj|mq, xn

Special Case: All Nodes Know Z

• Consider the case where the eavesdropper observes Z and node j ∈[1 : N]
observes (Xj, Z), which is a generalization of the special case for the 2-node
setup discussed earlier

• Theorem 9 [30]: The secret key capacity when all the nodes know Z is

CK = H(XN |Z) −RCFO([1 : N]|Z),

where RCFO([1 : N]|Z) is the CFO rate (see Lecture notes 21) conditioned on
all nodes knowing Z

• Achievability: The codebook generation for the public communications between
nodes follows that of the CFO problem. For the secret key codebook, for each
zn ∈T (n)
ǫ
, we randomly and independently partition the set of conditionally
typical Xn([1 : N]) sequences to 2nR bins. The index of the bin is the key to be
agreed upon

The nodes communicate to achieve omniscience using the protocol described in
the CFO achievability proof. That is, node j sends the bin index Mj of its
source sequence. For decoding, every node first reconstructs ˆXn([1 : N]) and
then sets the secret key equal to the bin index of ˆXn([1 : N])

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)

Secret Key Agreement: The Channel Model

• In the source model, it is assumed that nodes have access to correlated sources.
Where do these correlated sources come from?

• The channel model generalizes the source model by including a DM-WTC from
one of the nodes to the rest of the nodes (including the eavesdropper) in
addition to the noiseless public broadcast channel. The noisy channel channel is
used to generate the correlated sources at the nodes, which is used to generate
the secret key

• Communication in the channel model is performed in multiple rounds. Each
round consists of a number of transmissions over the DM-WTC followed by
rounds of interactive communication over the public channel. The definitions of
the secret key rate and achievability are similar to those of the source model

• Example [6]: Consider the BS-WTC(p1, p2) as defined before. Assume that the
Bob is allowed to feed back information to Alice through a public noiseless link
of unlimited capacity. This information is also available to Eve. Alice and Bob
wish to agree on a key for secure communication that the eavesdropper is
ignorant of. What is secret key capacity?

Maurer established the secrecy capacity using a wiretap channel coding scheme

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 61

over a virtual degraded BC from Bob to Alice Eve. Alice first sends a random
sequence Xn. Bob then randomly picks a key K ∈[1 : 2nR], adds its codeword
V n to the received sequence Y n, and sends the sum W n over the public
channel. Alice uses her knowledge of Xn to obtain a better version of V n than
Eve’s and proceeds to decode it to find ˆK

◦We now show that K satisfies the secrecy requirement. First we show that

1 ⊕Zn

= H(K|Zn ⊕W n) + H(W n|K, Zn ⊕W n) −H(W n|Zn ⊕W n)

= H(K|Zn ⊕W n)

This yields I(K; W n, Zn) = I(K; W n ⊕Zn)

a key rate of R < I(V ; V ⊕Z1) −I(V ; V ⊕Z1 ⊕Z2) −2δ
= H(p1 ∗p2) −H(p1) −2δ is achievable

• The optimality of the above coding scheme is proved using the general upper
bound on the secrecy capacity for channel model in [6]

CK-channel ≤min

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 64


• Lower bounds for the source model can be readily extended to the channel
model by restricting communication to one round consisting of n i.i.d.
transmissions over the DM-WTC to generate the correlated sources, followed by
interactive public communication to generate the key

This restriction turns the problem into an equivalent source model with sources
(X1, X2, . . . , XN, Z) ∼p(x1)p(x2, . . . , xN, z|x1), where p(x2, . . . , xN, z|x1) is
the DM-WTC from X1 to (X2, . . . , XN, Z). Thus, if RK(X1; . . . ; XN||Z) is a
lower bound on the key capacity for the source model with sources
(X1, X2, . . . , XN, Z), then

CK-channel ≥max
p(x1) RK(X1; . . . ; XN ||Z)

• Connection between Source Model and WTC [6]: Note that in Maurer’s
example we generated a key by constructing a virtual degraded wiretap channel
from X2 to (X, Z). This observation can be generalized to obtain the one-way
key rate for the source model from the wiretap channel result. We now show
achievability of the one-way key rate for the source model by converting it into a
WTC. For simplicity, we show the achievability of
RK−1 = I(X1; X2) −I(X1; Z). The general one-way key rate result can be
obtained as discussed in the section on one-way key rate

LNIT: Information Theoretic Secrecy (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page 23 – 65

Fix V ∼Unif(X1) independent of (X1, X2, Z). For i ∈[1 : n], node 1 turns the
public discussion channel into a wiretap channel by sending Vi ⊕X1i over it.
The equivalent WTC is then V →((X2, V ⊕X1), (Z, V ⊕X1)). From the
WTC result, we transmit a confidential message at a rate of

RK−1 = I(V ; X2, V ⊕X1) −I(V ; Z, V ⊕X1)

= I(V ; X2|V ⊕X1) −I(V ; Z|V ⊕X1)

= H(X2|V ⊕X1) −H(X2|V ⊕X1, V ) −H(Z|V ⊕X1) + H(Z|V ⊕X1, V )

(a)
= H(X2) −H(X2|X1, V ) −H(Z) + H(Z|X1, V )

= H(X2) −H(X2|X1) −H(Z) + H(Z|X1) = I(X1; X2) −I(X1; Z),

where (a) follows since V ⊕X1 is independent of X1 and hence (X2, Z) by
Markovity

• In [31] it is shown that if the eavesdropper’s sequence Zn is known to all nodes
(through the pubic channel), then

CK-channel = max
p(x1)(H(XN |Z) −RCFO([1 : N]|Z))

Note that this is an upper bound on the key capacity when the nodes do not
know Z. Generally tighter upper bounds are reported in [31, 32]

Page 23 – 66


Key New Ideas and Techniques

• Information theoretic notion of secrecy

• Randomized encoding

• Key generation from common randomness via public communication

Random Data Arrival at a DMC

• In the definitions of the channel coding problems, we assumed that data is
always available at the encoder. In many network applications, data is bursty
and it may or may not be available at the sender when the channel is free.
Moreover, the amount of data at the sender may exceed its finite queue size
resulting in data loss even before transmission

• It turns out that under fairly general data arrival models, if the data rate λ
bits/transmission is below the capacity C of the channel, the data can be sent
reliably to the receiver, while if λ ≥C, data cannot be reliably sent either
because the incoming data exceeds the sender’s queue size or because
transmission rate exceeds capacity

• We illustrate this general result using the following simple random data arrival
process. Assume data packets arrive at the sender according to an i.i.d. process
{Aλ(i)}, where the arrival rate λ is the product of the packet arrival rate
p ∈(0, 1] and packet size k bits. At the “end” of transmission time
i = 1, 2, . . . , A(i) = k bits, i.e., a packet arrives at the sender, with probability
p (the packet bits are modeled by k i.i.d. Bern(1/2) random variables), and
A(i) = 0, i.e., no packet arrives, with probability ¯p. The packets arriving at
different transmission times are assumed to be independent

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 2


• Define an augmented (2nR, n) block code for the DMC to consist of:

1. An augmented message set [1 : 2nR] ∪{0},

2. an encoder that assigns a codeword xn(m) to each m ∈[1 : 2nR] ∪{0}, and

3. a decoder that assigns a message ˆm ∈[1 : 2nR] ∪{0} or an error message e
to each received sequence yn

Mj
Xn
j
Encoder
Decoder
p(y|x)
Y n
j
ˆ
Mj

Queue

{Aλ(i)}

• The code is used in consecutive transmission blocks as follows. Let Q(i) be the
number of bits (backlog) in the sender’s queue at the “beginning” of time
i = 1, 2, . . .. At the beginning of time jn, j = 1, 2, . . ., i.e., at the beginning of
transmission block j, nR bits are taken out of the queue if Q(jn) ≥nR. The
bits are represented by a message Mj ∈[1 : 2nR] and the codeword xn(mj) is
sent over the DMC. If Q(jn) < nR, no bits are taken out of the queue and the
“0-message” codeword xn(0) is sent

• Thus, by the definition of the arrival time process,
Mj|{Mj ̸= 0} ∼Unif[1 : 2nR]

• The queue is said to be stable if supi E(Q(i)) ≤B for some constant B < ∞

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 3

• Lemma 1: If λ < R, then the queue is stable. Conversely, if the queue is stable,
then λ ≤R

The proof of this lemma is given in the Appendix

• Queue stability implies that we can make the probability of data loss as small as
desired with a finite buffer size (why?)

• Let pj be the probability that the sender queue has less than nR bits at the
beginning of transmission block j

• Define the probability of error in transmission block j as

P (n)

= pj P{ ˆ

• A rate R is said to be achievable if there exists a sequence of codes with
supj P (n)
ej
→0 as n →∞

• The data arriving at the encoder according to the process {Aλ(i)} can be
reliably communicated at rate R over the DMC if (i) the queue is stable, and
(ii) there exists a sequence of (2nR, n) codes with supj P (n)
ej
→0 as n →∞

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 4


• We wish to find necessary and sufficient conditions for reliable communication of
the data over the DMC

• Theorem 1: The random data arrival process {Aλ(i)} can be reliably
communicated over a DMC (X, p(y|x), Y) with capacity C if λ < C.
Conversely, given any sequence of (2nR, n) codes with supj P (n)
ej
→0 as
n →∞such that the queue is stable, then λ < R ≤C

• Achievability: If R < C, there exists a sequence of augmented (2nR, n) codes
with maximal probability of error →0 as n →∞. Using this sequence of codes
guarantees that P (n)
ej
→0 for every j

• Converse: We already know that we must have R > λ. Now, for each j,
nR = H(Mj|Mj ̸= 0)

≤I(Mj; Y n|Mj ̸= 0) + nǫn

≤n(C + ǫn)

• The above result can be extended to multiple user channels with random data
arrivals at each sender. For example consider the case of a DM-MAC with two
independent i.i.d. arrival processes {Aλ1(i)} and {Aλ2(i)}. The stability region
S for the two sender queues is the closure of the set of arrival rates (λ1, λ2)
such that both queues are stable

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 5

• We define the augmented code (2nR1, 2nR2, n), the average probability of error,
and achievability as for the DMC case

• Let C be the capacity of region for the DM-MAC, then it can be readily shown
that S = C

• Remark: The same result holds when the packets arrivals (but not the packet
contents) are correlated

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)

Asynchronous MAC

• In the single-hop channel models we discussed in Part II, we assumed that the
transmissions from the senders to the receivers are synchronized (both at the
symbol and block levels). In practice achieving such complete synchronization
may not be feasible. How does the lack of synchronization affect the capacity
region of the channel?

• We consider this question for the DM-MAC

capacity region is the same as for the synchronous case even when the
receiver does not know the delays

◦Totally asynchronous [2]: d = n −1, i.e., d1, d2 can vary from 0 to n −1. In
this case time-sharing becomes infeasible and the capacity region reduces to
the following:

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 9

Theorem 2 [2]: The capacity region for the asynchronous DM-MAC is the set
of all rate pairs (R1, R2) such that

R1 ≤I(X1; Y |X2),

R2 ≤I(X2; Y |X1),

R1 + R2 ≤I(X1, X2; Y )

for some p(x1)p(x2)

• Remark: The capacity region for the AWGN-MAC does not change with
asynchrony. However, one needs joint decoding to achieve all the points in the
capacity region

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 10

• We divide each n-transmission block into b + 1 subblocks each consisting of k

and send them in the following b subblocks

Note that the resulting rate pair for this code, (bR1/(b + 1), bR2/(b + 1)), can
be made arbitrarily close to (R1, R2) as b →∞

The (b + 1)k transmissions are divided into 4 groups:

◦Transmissions where both sender 1 messages and sender 2 messages are
correct. Each symbol in this group is generated according to
p(x1)p(x2)p(y|x1, x2). Assume there are k1 ∈[0 : (b + 1)k −1] such symbols

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 17

◦Transmissions where sender 1’s messages are in error but sender 2’s messages
are correct. Each symbol in this group is generated according to
p(x1)p(x2)p(y|x2). Assume there are k2 such symbols

◦Transmissions where sender 2’s messages are in error but sender 1’s messages
are correct. Each symbol in this group is generated according to
p(x1)p(x2)p(y|x1). Assume there are k3 such symbols

◦Transmissions where both sender 1 messages and sender 2 messages are in
error. Each symbol in this group is generated according to p(x1)p(x2)p(y).
Assume there are k4 such symbols

It is not difficult to see that k1 + k2 + k3 + k4 = (b + 1)k, k2 + k4 = k|J1|, and
k3 + k4 = k|J2|

To simplify the analysis of the probability of the event E(J1, J2), define the
event that no message is repeated in the block as

A := {m1j ̸= m1j′ for all j, j′ ∈[1 : b], j ̸= j′,

and ˜m2j ̸= ˜m2j′ for all j, j′ ∈[1 : b + 1], j ̸= j′}

Thus P(A) →1 as k →∞. We now bound P(E(J1, J2) ∩A)

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 18


Since all messages sent in the block are different, all erroneous messages must
be different (otherwise they would be detected and treated as part of event
E0 ∩A). Thus the symbol triples (X1i, X2i, Yi) are mutually independent for all
i ∈[d1 + δ + 1 : (b + 1)k + d1 + δ]. Using this independence and the joint
typicality lemma for each term, the probability of each erroneous message
pattern in subblocks J1, J2 is upper bounded by

2−k2(I(X1;Y |X2)−δ(ǫ)) · 2−k3(I(X2;Y |X1)−δ(ǫ)) · 2−k4(I(X1,X2;Y )−δ(ǫ))

The number of symbol patterns in the error locations is upper bounded by
2k(|J1|R1+|J2|R2). Thus, using the union of events bound, we obtain

P(E(J1, J2) ∩A)

≤2k(|J1|R1+|J2|R2) · 2−k2(I(X1;Y |X2)−δ(ǫ))−k3(I(X2;Y |X1)−δ(ǫ))−k4(I(X1,X2;Y )−δ(ǫ))

= 2−k2(I(X1;Y |X2)−R1−δ(ǫ)) · 2−k3(I(X2;Y |X1)−R2−δ(ǫ)) · 2−k4(I(X1,X2;Y )−R1−R2−δ(ǫ)),

which →0 as k →∞if R1 < I(X1; Y |X2) −δ(ǫ), R2 <
I(X2; Y |X1) −δ(ǫ), R1 + R2 < I(X1, X2; Y ) −δ(ǫ)

Bounding the probability of decoding error for mb
2 follows similarly. This
completes the proof of achievability

Random Access Channel [3]

• The random access channel (RAC) is a DM-MAC with state defined as
Yi = S1i · X1i ⊕S2i · X2i,
where {S1i} and {S2i} are independent Bern(p) processes

• This channel models packet collision in wireless multi-access network (Aloha []).
Sender j = 1, 2 has a packet to transmit (is active) when Sj = 1 and is inactive
when Sj = 0. The receiver knows which senders are active, but each sender
does not know whether the other sender is active

M1

M2

( ˆ

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 23

This model can be viewed as a special case of the fading DM-MAC [4] where
the receiver knows the complete channel state but each sender knows its own
state only. We consider the slow fading scenario

• The compound channel capacity region is R1 = R2 = 0

• The adaptive capacity region is the set of rate quadruples (R10, R11, R20, R21)
such that
R10 = min
s2 I(X1; Y |X2, s1 = 0, s2) = 0,

R20 = min
s1 I(X1; Y |X2, s1, s2 = 0) = 0,

R11 ≤min
s2 I(X1; Y |X2, s1 = 1, s2) ≤1,

R21 ≤min
s1 I(X1; Y |X2, s1, s2 = 1) ≤1,

R10 + R20 ≤I(X1, X2; Y |s1 = 0, s2 = 0) = 0,

R10 + R21 ≤I(X1, X2; Y |s1 = 0, s2 = 1) ≤1,

R11 + R20 ≤I(X1, X2; Y |s1 = 1, s2 = 0) ≤1,

R11 + R21 ≤I(X1, X2; Y |s1 = 1, s2 = 1) ≤1
for some p(x1)p(x2)

LNIT: Information Theory and Networking (2010-03-21 21:55)
Page 24 – 24


It is straightforward to see that X1 ∼Bern(1/2) and X2 ∼Bern(1/2) achieve
the upper bounds, and hence the adaptive capacity region reduces to the set of
rate quadruples (R10, R11, R20, R21) satisfying

R10 = R20 = 0, R11 ≤1, R21 ≤1

R10 + R20 = 0, R10 + R21 ≤1, R11 + R20 ≤1,

R11 + R21 ≤1

Since R10 = R20 = 0 and R11 = 1 −R21, the average adaptive sum-capacity is

p(1 −p)(R10 + R21) + p(1 −p)(R11 + R20) + p2(R11 + R21) = p(R11 + R21) = p

Therefore, allowing rate adaptation can greatly increase the average sum-rate

the chain has a unique limiting distribution and E( ˜Qj) converges to a limit [5]

Appendix A
Convex Sets and Functions

• Recall that a set R ⊆Rd is called convex if x, y ∈R implies that αx + ¯αy ∈R
for all α ∈[0, 1]. Thus, a convex set includes a line segment between any pair of
two points in the set. Consequently, a convex set includes every convex
combination x of any finite number of elements x1, . . . , xk, that is,

x =

k
�

j=1
αjxj

for some (α1, . . . , αk) such that αj ≥0, j ∈[1 : k], and �k
j=1 αj = 1. Thus, a
convex set includes every possible “average” among any tuple of points in the set

• The convex hull of a set R ⊆Rd is the union of all finite convex combinations
of elements in R. Equivalently, the convex hull of R is the smallest convex set
containing R

The convex closure of R is the closure of the convex hull of R, or equivalently,
the smallest closed convex set containing R

LNIT: Convex Sets and Functions (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page A – 1

• Fenchel–Eggleston–Carath´eodory Theorem [1]: Any point in the convex closure
of a connected compact set R ∈Rd can be represented as a convex
combination of at most d points in R

• Let R be a convex set and x0 be a point on its boundary. Suppose aTx ≤aTx0
for all x ∈R for some a ̸= 0. Then the hyperplane {y : aT(x −x0) = 0} is
referred to as a supporting hyperplane to R at the point x0. The supporting
hyperplane theorem [1] states that at least one such hyperplane exists

• Any closed bounded convex set R can be described as the interesection of
closed half spaces characterized by supporting hyperplanes. This provides a
condition to check if two convex sets are identical

Lemma A.1 [2]: Let R ⊆Rd be convex. Let R1 ⊆R2 be two bounded convex
subsets of R, closed relative to R. If every supporting hyperplane of R2
intersects with R1, then R1 = R2

• Sometimes it is easier to consider supporting hyperplanes of the smaller set

Lemma A.2: Let R ⊆Rd be convex. Let R1 ⊆R2 be two bounded convex
subsets of R, closed relative to R. Let A be a subset of boundary points of R1
such that the convex hull of A includes all boundary points. If each x0 ∈A has
a unique supporting hyperplane and lies on the boundary of R2, then R1 = R2

LNIT: Convex Sets and Functions (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page A – 2


• Convex function: A real-valued function g(x) is called convex if the epigraph
{(x, a) : g(x) ≤a} is convex for all a. If g(x) is twice differentiable, it is convex
iff

∇2g(x) =
�∂2g(x)

∂xi∂xj

�

ij
is positive semidefinite for all x

If −g(x) is convex, then g(x) is called concave

• Examples

◦Entropy: g(x) = −�

j xj log xj is concave

◦Log determinant: log |X| is concave on the positive definite matrices X

◦Maximum: sup{gθ} of any collection of convex functions is convex

LNIT: Convex Sets and Functions (2010-03-20 20:38)
Page A – 3

References

[1] H. G. Eggleston, Convexity.
Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1958.

[2] R. T. Rockafellar, Convex Analysis.
Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 1970.

Functional Representation Lemma

• The following lemma shows that any conditional pmf can be represented as a
function of the input and an independent random variable

• Functional Representation Lemma [5]: Let (X, Y, Z) ∼p(x, y, z). Then, we can
represent Z as a function of (Y, W) for some random variable W of cardinality
|W| ≤|Y|(|Z| −1) + 1 such that W is independent of Y and
X →(Y, Z) →W form a Markov chain

• Proof:

◦It suffices to show that Z can be represented as a function of (Y, W) for
some W independent of Y with |W| ≤|Y|(|Z| −1) + 1. The Markovity
X →(Y, Z) →W is guaranteed by generating X according to the conditional
pmf p(x|y, z), which results in the desired joint pmf p(x, y, z) on (X, Y, Z)

◦We first illustrate the proof through the following example

Suppose (Y, Z) is a DBSP(p) for some p < 1/2, i.e.,
pZ|Y (0|0) = pZ|Y (1|1) = 1 −p and pZ|Y (0|1) = pZ|Y (1|0) = p

Let P := {F(z|y) : (y, z) ∈Y × Z} be the set of all values the conditional
cdf F(z|y) takes and let W be a random variable independent of Y with cdf

LNIT: Probability and Estimation (2010-03-21 22:00)
Page B – 4


F(w) such that {F(w) : w ∈W} = P as shown in the figure below

Mean Squared Error Estimation

• Let (X, Y) ∼F(x, y). The minimum mean squared error (MMSE) estimate of
X given Y is a (measurable) function ˆx(Y) of Y that minimizes the mean
squared error (MSE) E((X −ˆX)2)

• To find the MMSE estimate, note that E(Xg(Y)) = E(E(X|Y)g(Y)) for every
g(Y). Hence, the MSE is lower bounded by

E((X −ˆX)2) = E((X −E(X|Y) + E(X|Y) −ˆX)2)

= E((X −E(X|Y))2) + E((E(X|Y) −ˆX)2)

≥E((X −E(X|Y))2)

Thus, the MMSE estimate of X given Y is the conditional expectation E(X|Y)
and the corresponding MSE is

E(X −E(X|Y))2 = E(X2) −E((E(X|Y))2) = E(Var(X|Y))

Note that
Var(X) = E(Var(X|Y)) + Var(E(X|Y)),

LNIT: Probability and Estimation (2010-03-21 22:00)
Page B – 7

• The linear MMSE estimate of X given Y = (Y1, . . . , Yn) is a linear (or affine)
function ˆX = aTY + b that minimizes the MSE

◦For simplicity we first consider the case E(X) = 0 and E(Y) = 0

Orthogonality Principle: The linear MMSE estimate of X given Y is
ˆX = aTY,

where a is such that the estimation error (X −ˆX) is orthogonal to Y, that is,

E((X −aTY)Yi) = 0 for every i ∈[1 : n],

or equivalently,
aTKY = KXY

If KY is nonsingular, then the linear MMSE estimate is
ˆX = KXYK−1

LNIT: Probability and Estimation (2010-03-21 22:00)
Page B – 8


◦When X or Y has a nonzero mean, the linear MMSE estimate is determined
by finding the MMSE estimate of X′ = X −E(X) given Y′ = Y −E(Y)
and setting ˆX = ˆX′ + E(X). Thus, if KY is nonsingular, then the linear
MMSE estimate of X given Y is

Gaussian Random Vectors

• Let X = (X1, . . . , Xn) be a random vector with mean µ and covariance matrix
K ⪰0. We say that (X1, . . . , Xn) is jointly Gaussian and X is a Gaussian
random vector if every linear combination aTX is a Gaussian random variable.
If K ≻0, then the joint pdf is well-defined as

f(x) =

• A Gaussian random vector X = (X1, . . . , Xn) satisfies the following properties:

1. If X1, . . . , Xn are uncorrelated (i.e., K is diagonal), then X1, . . . , Xn are
independent

This can be verified by substituting Kij = 0 for all i ̸= j in the joint pdf

2. Linear transformation of a GRV yields a GRV, i.e., given any m × n matrix
A, then
Y = AX ∼N(Aµ, AKAT)

This can be verified from the characteristic function of Y

3. Marginals of X are Gaussian, i.e., X(S) is jointly Gaussian for any subset
S ⊆[1 : n]

LNIT: Probability and Estimation (2010-03-21 22:00)
Page B – 11

This follows from the second property

4. Conditionals of X are Gaussian, more specifically, if

Cardinality Bounding Techniques

• Here we introduce a general technique [1] for bounding the cardinalities of
auxiliary random variables.

• We use the following lemma, which is a direct consequence of
Fenchel–Eggleston–Carath´eodry Theorem in Appendix A:

of pmfs on X and p(x|u) be a collection of conditional pmfs on X for every
u ∈U . Let gj, j = 1, . . . , d, be real-valued continuous functions on P(X).

p(x|u′) on X for every u′ ∈U ′ such that for j = 1, . . . , d,

j = |Y2|,
I(X; Y1|U = u),
j = |Y2| + 1

Again from the lemma, there exists U ′ (in general different from the one
above) with |U′| ≤|Y2| + 1 such that p(y2) = EU′[pY2|U(y2|U ′)], and
H(Y2|U ′), I(X; Y1|U ′), and H(Y2) stay the same as with the original U.
Hence R(U, X) = R(U ′, X)

◦We can show that R(U, X) = R(U ′, X) for some U ′ with |U′| ≤|Y1| + 1 in
the exactly same manner, but by fixing p(y1) instead of p(y2). The physical
degradedness of the channel guarantees that p(y1) determines H(Y2)

◦Combining the above three steps, we have shown that for every U there
exists a U ′ with |U′| ≤min{|X|, |Y1|, |Y2|} + 1 and R(U, X) = R(U ′, X).
This establishes the desired cardinality bound on U

Page C – 4


• This technique can be extended to give cardinality bounds for capacity regions
with two or more auxiliary random variables [2, 3]

As an example, consider the 3-receiver degraded DM-BC p(y1, y2, y3|x). The
capacity region is the set of rate triples (R1, R2, R3) such that

R1 ≤I(X; Y1|V ),

R2 ≤I(V ; Y2|U),

R3 ≤I(U; Y3)

for some p(u)p(v|u)p(x|v)

We show that it suffices to take |U| ≤|X| + 2 and |V| ≤(|X| + 1)(|X| + 2)

◦First consider the |X| + 2 continuous functions of p(v|u):

p(x|u),

I(X; Y1|V, U = u) = I(X; Y1|U = u) −I(V ; Y1|U = u),

I(V ; Y2|U = u),

H(Y3|U = u)

As in the 2-receiver DM-BC example (the bound |U| ≤|Y2| + 1), there exists
U ′ with |U′| ≤|X| + 2 such that p(x), I(X; Y1|V ) = I(X; Y1|V, U),

LNIT: Cardinality Bounding Techniques (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page C – 5

I(V ; Y2|U), I(U; Y3) are preserved. Let V ′ denote the corresponding random
variable

◦Now for each u′ ∈|U′|, consider |X| + 1 continuous functions of p(x|v′, u′):
p(x|v′, u′), I(V ′; Y1|U ′ = u′), H(Y2|V = v′, U ′ = u′). Then as in the
2-receiver DM-BC example (the bound |U| ≤|X| + 1), for each u′, there
exists V ′′|{U ′ = u′} ∼p(v′′|u′) such that the support of p(v′′|u′) is ≤|X| + 1
and that p(x|u′), I(X; Y1|V ′, U ′ = u′), I(V ′; Y2|U ′ = u′) are preserved

◦By relabeling the support of p(v′′|u′) for each u′ ∈U′ and redefining
p(x|v′′, u′) accordingly, we can construct (U ′, V ′′) with |U′| ≤|X| + 2 and
|V′′| ≤|X| + 1 with all three mutual information terms same as before

However, this choice does not satisfy the Markov condition U ′ →V ′′ →X in
general!

◦Instead, we use V ′′′ = (U ′, V ′′) (without relabeling). Then
|V′′′| ≤|U′| · |V′′| ≤(|X| + 1)(|X| + 2) and U ′ →V ′′′ →X →(Y1, Y2, Y3)

LNIT: Cardinality Bounding Techniques (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page C – 6


form a Markov chain. Furthermore,

I(X; Y1|V ′′′) = I(X; Y1|U ′) −I(V ′′′; Y1|U ′)

= I(X; Y1|U ′) −I(V ′′; Y1|U ′)

= I(X; Y1|U ′) −I(V ′; Y1|U ′)

= I(X; Y1|U) −I(V ; Y1|U)

= I(X; Y1|V )

Similarly

I(V ′′′; Y2|U ′) = I(V ′′; Y2|U ′) = I(V ′; Y2|U ′) = I(V ; Y2|U),

I(U ′; Y3) = I(U; Y3)

This completes the proof of cardinality bound

• Remarks:

◦We can apply the same technique to bound the cardinality of the time-sharing
random variable Q appearing the characterization of the DM-MAC capacity

(R1, R2) such that

◦This technique, however, does not provide cardinality bounds for all

Appendix D
Fourier–Motzkin Elimination

• Suppose R ⊆Rd is the set of tuples (r1, r2, . . . , rd) satisfying a finite system of
linear inequalities Ard ≤bk:

a11r1 + a12r2 + · · · + a1drd ≤b1

a21r1 + a22r2 + · · · + a2drd ≤b2
...
≤...

ak1r1 + ak2r2 + · · · + akdrd ≤bk

Such R is called a polyhedron (or a polytope if it is bounded)

• Let R′ ⊆Rd−1 be the projection of R on the hyperplane {r1 = 0} (or any
hyperplane parallel to it). In other words:

1. If (r1, r2, . . . , rd) ∈R, then (r2, . . . , rd) ∈R′

2. If (r2, . . . , rd) ∈R′, then there exists an r1 such that (r1, r2, . . . , rd) ∈R

Thus R′ captures the exact collection of inequalities satisfied by (r2, . . . , rd)

LNIT: Fourier–Motzkin Elimination (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page D – 1

• Question: How can we find the system of linear inequalities that characterize R′

from the original system of k inequalities for R ?

• The Fourier–Motzkin elimination (see, e.g., [1]) gives a systematic procedure to
finding the system of linear inequalities in (r2, . . . , rd)

• As an example, consider the system of inequalities given by

−r1
≤−1
−2r2 ≤−1
−r1 +r2 ≤0
−r1 −r2 ≤−2
+r1 +4r2 ≤15
+2r1 −r2 ≤3
r1

r2

Note that r2 ∈R′ if and only if there exists r1 (for given r2) that satisfies all
inequalities, which in turn happens if and only if every lower bound on r1 is ≤
every upper bound on r1. Hence, the sufficient and necessary condition for
r2 ∈R′ becomes

max{1, r2, 2 −r2} ≤min{(r2 + 3)/2, 15 −4r2}

LNIT: Fourier–Motzkin Elimination (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page D – 2


(6 inequalities total) as well as the inequality −2r2 ≤−1 in the original system
that does not involve r1
Solving for r2 and removing redudant inequalities, it can be easily checked that
R′ = {r2 : 1/2 ≤r2 ≤3}

• Now the extension of this method to the d-dimensional case is straightforward:

1. Write the system of inequalities into three categories: 1) the ones without r1,
i.e., aj1 = 0, 2) upper bounds on r1, i.e., aj1 > 0, 3) lower bounds on r1, i.e.,
aj1 < 0. Here each upper or lower bound is an affine equation in (r2, . . . , rd)

2. The first category is copied verbatim for R′ as inequalities in (r2, . . . , rd)

3. Then we generate a new system of inequalities by writing each lower bound ≤
each upper bound

Thus we have a new system of inequalities in (r2, . . . , rd)

LNIT: Fourier–Motzkin Elimination (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page D – 3

• Remarks:

◦The number of inequalities can increase rapidly—in the worst case, there can
be k2/4 inequalities generated from original k inequalities. Some inequalities
can be inactive (i.e., redundant)

◦We can project to an arbitrary hyperplane by taking an affine transformation
of variables

◦We can eliminate multiple variables by applying the method successively

◦This method can be applied to “symbolic” inequalities as well. In this case,
we can consider (r1, . . . , rd, b1, . . . , bk) as the set of variables and eliminate
unnecessary variables among r1, . . . , rd. In particular, if the constants
b1, . . . , bk are information quantities, inequality relations among them can be
further incorporated to remove inactive inequalities (see [2] for an example)

◦The Fourier–Motzkin procedure can be performed easily by computer
programs such as PORTA [3]

LNIT: Fourier–Motzkin Elimination (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page D – 4


Example: Han–Kobayashi Inner Bound

• Suppose we wish to eliminate R01 and R02 in the system of inequalities:

R1 −R01 ≤I1,

R1 ≤I2,

R1 −R01 + R02 ≤I3,

R1 + R02 ≤I4,

R2 −R02 ≤I5,

R2 ≤I6,

R2 −R02 + R01 ≤I7,

R2 + R01 ≤I8,

• Step 1 (elimination of R01)

We have two upper bounds on R01:

R01 ≤I7 −R2 + R02,

R01 ≤I8 −R2

LNIT: Fourier–Motzkin Elimination (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page D – 5

and two lower bounds on R01:

R01 ≥R1 −I1,

R01 ≥R1 + R02 −I3

Comparing upper and lower bounds, and copying four inequalities in the original
system that do not involve R01, we obtain a new system of inequalities in
(R02, R1, R2) given by

R1 ≤I2,

R1 + R02 ≤I4,

R2 −R02 ≤I5,

R2 ≤I6,

R1 + R2 −R02 ≤I1 + I7,

R1 + R2 ≤I1 + I8,

R1 + R2 ≤I3 + I7,

R1 + R2 + R02 ≤I3 + I8

Noting that none of these 8 inequalities is redundant, we move on to eliminate
R02 from them

LNIT: Fourier–Motzkin Elimination (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page D – 6


• Step 2 (elimination of R02)

Comparing upper bounds on R02
R02 ≤I4 −R1,
R02 ≤I3 + I8 −R1 −R2
with lower bounds
R02 ≥R2 −I5,
R02 ≥R1 + R2 −I1 −I7,

and copying inequalities that do not involve R20, we obtain

R1 ≤I2,

R2 ≤I6,

R1 + R2 ≤I1 + I8,

R1 + R2 ≤I3 + I7,

R1 + R2 ≤I4 + I5,

2R1 + R2 ≤I1 + I4 + I7,

R1 + 2R2 ≤I3 + I5 + I8,

2R1 + 2R2 ≤I1 + I3 + I7 + I8

Finally we note that the last inequality is redundant since it is implied by the
third and fourth inequalities; thus we have 7 inequalities on (R1, R2)

Appendix E
Convex Optimization

• We review basic results in convex optimization. For details, please refer to [1]

• An optimization problem

minimize g0(x)

subject to gj(x) ≤0, j ∈[1 : k],

Ax = b

is convex if gj, j ∈[0 : k] are convex. We denote by
D = {x : gj(x) ≤0, j ∈[1 : k], Ax = b} the set of feasible points (domain of
the optimization problem). The convex optimization problem is feasible if
D ̸= ∅. The optimal value of the problem is denoted by
p∗= inf{g0(x) : x ∈D} (or −∞if the problem is infeasible). Any x that
attains the infimum is optimal and denoted by x∗

LNIT: Convex Optimization (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page E – 1

• Examples

◦Linear programming (LP):

minimize cTx

subject to Ax = b,

xj ≥0 for all j

See, for example, [2] for more details

◦Differential entropy maximization under correlation constraint [3, 4]:

maximize log |K|

subject to Kj,j+k = ak, k ∈[0 : l]

Note that this problem is a special case of matrix determininant maximization
with linear matrix inequalities (max-det) [5]

• Lagrangian dual problem: We define the Lagrangian associated with a feasible
optimization problem

minimize g0(x)

subject to gj(x) ≤0, j ∈[1 : k],

Ax = b

LNIT: Convex Optimization (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page E – 2


as

L(x, λ, ν) = g0(x) +

k
�

j=1
λjgj(x) + νT(Ax −b)

We further define the Lagrangian dual function (or dual function in short) as

φ(λ, ν) = inf
x L(x, λ, ν)

It can be easily seen that for any (λ, ν) with λj ≥0 for all j and any feasible x,

φ(λ, ν) ≤g0(x)

This leads to the following (Lagrange) dual problem:

maximize φ(λ, ν)

subject to λj ≥0, j ∈[1 : k]

Note that φ(λ, ν) is concave, hence the dual problem is convex (regardless of
whether the primal problem is convex or not). The optimal value of the dual
problem is denoted by d∗and the dual optimal point is denoted by (λ∗, ν∗)

The original optimization problem and its feasible set and optimal value are
sometimes referred to as the primal problem, primal feasible set, and primal
optimal value

LNIT: Convex Optimization (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page E – 3

• Strong duality: From the definition of the dual function and dual problem, we
already know the following lower bound on the primal optimal value:

d∗≤p∗

When the bound is tight (i.e., d∗= p∗), we say that strong duality holds. One
simple condition for strong duality is as follows:

Slater’s condition: If the primal problem is convex and there exists a feasible x
in the relative interior of D, that is, gj(x) < 0, j ∈[1 : k] and Ax = b, then
strong duality holds

• KKT condition: If the strong duality holds (say, Slater’s condition holds), then

g0(x∗) = φ(λ∗, ν∗)

≤inf
x g0(x) +

k

k

≤g0(x∗)

LNIT: Convex Optimization (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page E – 5

Following equality conditions, we obtain the following sufficient and necessary
condition (commonly referred to as the KKT condition) for the primal optimal
point x∗and dual optimal point (λ∗, ν∗):

◦x∗minimizes L(x, λ∗, ν∗). This condition can be easily checked if
L(x, λ∗, ν∗) is differentiable

◦Complementary slackness: λ∗
jgj(x∗) = 0, j ∈[1 : k]

• Example: Consider the following determinant maximization problem in X:

maximize log |X + K|

subject to X ⪰0,

tr(X) ≤P,

where K is a given positive definite matrix

Noting that X ⪰0 iff tr(ΥX) ≥0 for every Υ ⪰0, we form the Lagrangian

L(X, Υ, λ) = log |X + K| + tr(ΥX) −λ(tr(X) −P)

Since the domain D has nonempty interior (for example, X = (P/2)I is
feasible), Slater’s condition is satisfied and strong duality holds. Hence the KKT
condition characterizes the optimal X∗and (Υ∗, λ∗)

LNIT: Convex Optimization (2010-03-20 20:39)
Page E – 6


◦X∗maximizes the Lagrangian. Since (d/dY ) log |Y | = Y −1 for Y ≻0 and
(d/dY ) tr(AY ) = A (see [1] for more details of matrix differentiation),

∂
∂XL(X, Υ∗, λ∗) = (X + K)−1 + Υ∗−λI = 0

◦Complementary slackness:

tr(Υ∗X∗) = 0

λ∗(tr(X∗) −P) = 0
